Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Tanker attacks, GOM shut-ins support oil even as Trump cools Iran escalation fears
Trump says US will not attack Iran before November midterms
Tanker attacks near Qatar disrupt shipping
Tropical Storm Isaias shuts in 1.28 million b/d Gulf crude output
Stepped-up Iranian attacks on shipping and Gulf of Mexico production shut-ins pushed crude prices higher Oct. 8, but contracts settled off session highs after US President Donald Trump said US forces would not attack Iran before the November elections.
NYMEX November WTI settled $3.21/barrel higher at $91.49/b, and ICE December Brent climbed $4.08/b to $104.28/b.
"[The US] will not be attacking Iran at any time prior to the Midterm Elections to be held in the United States on November 3rd," Trump said in an Oct. 8 post on his social media platform Truth Social. The US is engaged in "productive discussions" with Iran and that "oil is flowing in record numbers" through the Strait of Hormuz, Trump said, adding that 22 million barrels of oil transited the strait last night.
Trump's post ran counter to recent media reports stating the White House had directed the Pentagon to prepare for potential strikes on Iran ahead of the elections that had boosted crude prices overnight.
Still, regional tensions and uncertainty continue to support hefty risk premiums for crude markets, analysts said.
"While flows from the region have largely normalized, the need for US navy escorts, increased costs and logistical frictions and

International Crude Johan Sverdrup collapses further, freight complicates November trade FOB Mongstad differential falls $11/b week on week High freight could curtail US SPR sour flows Latin American sellers could prioritize shorter-haul trades
to Europe
Differentials for Norway's Johan Sverdrup have fallen over $11/b across five pricing sessions with prompt cargoes under pressure from cheaper alternative availabilities and a steeply backwardated market structure.
However, surging freight rates across the Atlantic basin have complicated the supply balance ahead, leaving participants struggling to value cargoes for the November trading cycle.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the differential for Johan Sverdrup FOB Mongstad at a $3.245/b premium over Dated Brent Oct. 8, down $2.76/b on the day and $11.13/b across the previous five pricing sessions.
Values for October-loading prompt cargoes have come off in recent sessions with alternative availabilities from the Middle East, including Iraqi Basrah Medium crude, landing competitively into the region.
"Johan Sverdrup has suffered downward pressure as [Iraqi] Basrah Medium arrival offers were cheap, so the spread between heavy grades and lighter crudes such as the Dated Brent basket grades seems quite high," a Europe-based crude trader said.
Values for November-arrivals of Basrah Medium delivered into the Mediterranean were reported by traders at premiums of between 50 cents/b to $2/b over November Dated Brent in the

week to Oct. 8. "Sours are going down, Johan Sverdrup needs to soften even
more to compete with Basrah," said a second trader. A steep backwardation between the Brent CFDs settling across
October and November has also contributed to demonstrated pressure on prompt October-loading cargoes, said traders.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process has seen offers for October-loading Johan Sverdrup FOB Mongstad cargoes across the Sept. 28 to Oct. 8 pricing sessions, with a cargo eventually trading in the Oct. 8 session at a $2.70/b premium over Dated Brent.
Freight complicates November cycle Market participants had originally anticipated a bearish
November cycle for European sour crudes, with the release of sour barrels from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve announced Sept. 29 expected to lengthen balances significantly.
However, surging freight rates across the Atlantic basin have complicated the supply picture ahead, possibly preventing SPR barrels from reaching Europe.
"The US SPR tender is ongoing still but I think given it's sour and freight is prohibitively expensive, it's likely to be unawarded or to stay domestic," said a third trader. "[However], the SPR barrels are all 620,000 to 680,000-barrel parcels on Aframaxes so if it does get exported at all, it will be more for Europe."
While SPR flows could be curtailed, traders were expecting more Latin American flows in the cycle ahead, with producers across the Americas expected to prioritize Europe as an export destination to save on longer-haul freight costs to Asia.
This could result in lengthening balances, but with competing barrels landing at elevated premiums -- potentially providing a floor to Johan Sverdrup differentials.
"Freight alone is over $20/b, so landed [values] for Guyana crudes are already mid $15-17/b range," said a fourth trader. "For now Johan Sverdrup around $7/b FOB is still much cheaper versus delivered values into Rotterdam."
With cross-UK Continent freight also at record high values and expected to pressure FOB differentials, liquidity has ground to a halt as market participants struggled to value barrels in the November cycle.
"JS is not trading, nobody knows how to value anything with freight into the region so high, but FOB differentials are also in freefall," said a fifth trader.
Regardless, preliminary value pegs for November-loading cargoes have emerged at higher levels than shown for Octoberloaders, with values between $8/b to $10/b heard in the week to Oct. 8.
-- Natasha Tan
Mediterranean sweet crude differentials fall on high freight, excess supply
Expensive freight closes arbitrages, lengthens Med supply
Producers struggle to pass on freight costs to receivers
Demand under pressure from declining refining margins

North African and Kazakh sweet crude traded in the Mediterranean has come under pressure in recent days as closed arbitrages trapped more barrels in the region at a time when falling product cracks trimmed refining margins, dampening demand.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed CPC Blend Suezmax CIF Augusta at a $3.255/barrel premium to Dated Brent Oct. 8 after differentials for the Kazakh crude fell from a high of $6/b Sept. 18, when the closure of Saudi Arabia's East-West Pipeline lifted prices in the European crude complex. The CPC differential has average minus $1.02/b so far this year, Platts data showed.
"CPC is now more headache than joy," one Mediterranean trader said earlier this week, adding that expensive freight made it very difficult to move product as any delays could be very costly.
Platts assessed the Dirty Black Sea-Med 135,000-metric-ton freight at w995 on Oct. 7, nearly double from w500 on Sept. 28, effectively closing arbitrages to Northwest Europe and Asia and even making Med shipments more challenging.
"The market is waiting to see the freight coming off before any oil is moved," a second source said. "Now the arbitrage to NWE is impossible."
North African FOB grades assessed by Platts, Libya's Es Sider and Algeria's Saharan Blend, have also fallen in recent days, driven down by expensive freight and worsening refining margins, market sources said.
"With Aframax freight in the Mediterranean at around w800, it is wishful thinking from North African producers to try and pass on the cost of freight to consumers," a third Med trader said, adding that prices would have to fall to attract more interest.
The Saharan Blend FOB differential to Dated Brent was assessed at $4.60/b Oct. 7, down 35 cents/b on the day and more than $3/b below the $6.70/b assessment Sept. 21, Platts data showed. Es Sider saw a similar trend, with prices falling from $6.75/b to $5.25/b over the same period.
"Saharan Blend needs to fall further to be able to compete with WTI Midland in Rotterdam; these levels are not sustainable with the current competition in the Med," a fourth Med source said.
The pressures have been exacerbated further by falling refining margins, which have pushed FOB differentials down even more.
"Margins are no longer great, and the FOB differential has to crash," a fifth Med trader said.
-- Jan Kedzior
Azeri Light differentials at record high amid elevated diesel cracks
Azeri surges as CPC Blend lags
Diesel-rich crudes continue to attract premiums
Tight fundamentals support diesel prices
Azeri Light differentials reached the highest level since records began Oct. 7 as elevated middle distillate cracks continue

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

to support buying appetite for the diesel-rich Azeri grade. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Azeri Light CIF
Augusta at $16.50/barrel premium to Dated Brent Oct. 7, above the previous peak of $16/b Sept. 22 and $13/b on July 15, 2022, when Azeri Light rallied as Europe lost the supply of dieselrich Urals.
"Azeri Light is able to hold value because it's so rich in diesel," a Mediterranean trader said. The diesel/heating oil yield of Azeri Light is 25% by volume, compared with 21% for Es Sider and Saharan Blend, which are the competing sweet grades from the region.
The high diesel yield is matched by WTI Midland, which has seen a steep rise in differentials after the East-West pipeline closed Sept. 11.
Between Sept. 11 and Oct. 7, WTI Midland DAP Augusta differential to Dated Brent jumped $10.30/b to $23.20/b while Azeri Light rose $8.2/b to $16.50/b over the same period. Both grades outpaced the delivered CPC Blend, which gained $4.70/b to $3.255/b on Oct. 7, as lower diesel yields and ample supply kept pressure on the Kazakh grade.
"Such a massive range these days. The Azeri/CPC spread would usually be $2-5/b", another Med source said.
This comes as diesel prices in the Mediterranean remain elevated against a backdrop of constrained supply and bullish market sentiment.
The flat price rose in September following reports that US President Donald Trump was mulling a US diesel export ban, with Platts assessing 10 ppm diesel CIF Med cargoes at $1,664.75/mt

Sept. 15, the highest level on record, Platts data showed. While prices have eased amid the G7's commitment to release
up to 100 million barrels of oil stocks over the next four months, they remain at historically high levels. Platts assessed ULSD CIF Med cargoes at $1,422/mt on Oct. 7, down $66/mt week over week, but much above the 2025 average of $692.18/mt, Platts data showed.
Diesel prices remain supported by uncertainty over the pace of stock release and tight fundamentals.
"Prices could come off quite significantly when the stocks are released," said one European diesel trader, but cautioned that "other releases have been slow to reach the market ... and the question is whether the tenders go ahead."
This all comes on top of a Russian diesel export ban, which has removed over 1 million mt of product from the Med each month since its implementation, leaving the region scrambling for alternative supply routes and supporting diesel cracks.
-- Jan Kedzior, Sophia Aung

ADNOC sets Nov Murban crude OSP differential at $11/b premium to Platts Dubai
First OSP differentials against Platts Dubai since switch from IFAD Murban
Lifting on FOB Fujairah, STS Fujairah-Sohar range basis

Abu Dhabi National Oil Co. has set the November official selling price differential for its flagship Murban crude at an $11/b premium against the average of Platts Dubai crude assessments for the month of loading, according to a company notice seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 8.
These are the first of ADNOC's OSPs to be priced against Platts Dubai since its shift away from the IFAD Murban benchmark.
The November OSP differential for Umm Lulu was set at a $2/b premium against Platts Dubai, while that for Das Blend was set at a $1/b premium against Platts Dubai, and for Upper Zakum at a 50 cents/b premium against Platts Dubai, the notice said.
The grades are all for lifting on an FOB Fujairah loading terminal and FOB/STS Fujairah-Sohar range basis, the notice said.
ADNOC, in a July 31 notice, said it would adopt a promptmonth pricing methodology based on the Platts Dubai benchmark for its crude official selling prices, effective Nov. 1.
Prior to the switch, its OSPs had used an ICE Futures Abu Dhabi-based pricing methodology, which uses the Murban futures contract and priced crude two months ahead of loading.

UK sanctions more Russian oil companies, shadow fleet tanker
Oil companies Zarubezhneft and INK Capital sanctioned
New listings include 12 new shadow fleet tankers
UK now sanctions 90% of Russian oil production capacity
The UK has sanctioned the Russian oil companies Zarubezhneft and INK Capital, along with a dozen new "shadow tankers" involved in evading restrictions, it announced on Oct. 8, as it seeks new routes to mount pressure on Moscow over its four-year war with Ukraine.
A statement from the UK's foreign office said that by listing the two Russian oil companies, it was cutting off revenue sources for the country's war economy, as well as closing avenues for larger energy companies to obscure their operations and avoid existing measures.
The addition of 12 more oil tankers to the UK sanctions list was additionally designed to prevent the continued trade of Russian oil above the UK's price cap, which sets a $44.10/b threshold for the maximum value at which Russian crude can be legally traded. The additional shadow tanker sanctions takes the total number of listed vessels to over 600, the statement said.
The new listings follow a raft of previous UK sanctions, which already cover the majority of the Russian oil sector and a vast proportion of the shipping network used to support its trade. With its existing sanctions on Rosneft, Gazprom, Lukoil, Surgutneftegas and Tatneft, the UK was the first G7 member to

sanction Russia's four leading oil companies. The state-owned Zarubezhneft has upstream oil and gas
projects in Russia, Vietnam, Indonesia, Uzbekistan, Cuba and Egypt, the largest of which are run by its Vietsovpetro and Rusvietpetro joint ventures. The former focuses on the continental shelf of southern Vietnam, while Rusvietro produces roughly 60,000 b/d of oil from 14 northern Russian fields.
S&P Global Commodities at Sea data detected six tankers shipped by Zarubezhneft in 2026, which delivered a total of 3.8 million barrels of Urals crude to Turkish and Indian refineries from the Baltic port of Primorsk.
In Bosnia and Herzegovina, the company also has a significant downstream footprint, operating a refinery in Brod and a motor oil plant in the nearby town of Modrica. Through its subsidiary Nestro Petrol, the company operates 86 fuel stations in Bosnia and Herzegovina focused mostly on LPG and compressed natural gas, while a separate entity, OPTIMA Grupa, focuses on Balkans trading.
INK Capital is the main holding company of Russia's Irkutsk Oil Company, an independent producer with oil and gas projects in Eastern Siberia's Irkutsk, Krasnoyarsk and Republic of Sakha regions. In 2025, it also commissioned a 650,000 mt/year polymer plant, which processes Eastern Siberian feedstock to produce high-density polyethylene (HDPE) and linear low-density polyethylene (LLDPE).
Neither of the companies was immediately available for comment.
With its new bundle of designations, the UK government estimates its sanctions now cover over 90% of Russia's total oil production capacity, while tanker listings remain a focus to prevent Russia from capitalizing on surging energy prices.
"Through this action, we're not only targeting ships already on the water, but going after those who facilitate the supply chain used to move Russia's oil around the world," the government said in its statement.
Previous sanctions waiver The new targets mirror previous actions by the UK
government, which has recently broadened its focus from the energy sector to target more financial networks and companies involved in sectors such as drone manufacturing. It has previously floated even tougher energy restrictions, but has recently rowed back on escalation due to domestic price concerns.
Westminster has previously aligned closely with the EU in its efforts to amplify pressure on Russia, and in February offered its support for a full maritime services ban that was ultimately abandoned by the EU. In a policy mirroring EU law, the UK was also due to clamp down on imports of fuel made from Russian crude in third countries like India and Turkey, but later opted to defer a full-fledged ban.
The UK formally implemented its "refining loophole" ban in May, but issued a separate waiver exempting its two largest oil product imports, diesel and jet fuel. After significant media backlash over the relaxed Russian restrictions, the government

IEA's extra oil release would come only after implementing existing obligations: Sadamori
Diesel, jet fuel likely be focus for remaining 100 mil barrels release
Asia exceeds IEA oil release quotas; Europe lags at 60%
Winter LNG outlook improves with new supply
The International Energy Agency's extra oil release would come only after the implementation of existing obligations, Keisuke Sadamori, the IEA's former director of energy markets and security, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 8.
"In order for the additional releases, they have to implement the existing obligations first," said Sadamori, who retired from the IEA on June 30, on the sidelines of the 13th Annual Meeting of the Innovation for Cool Earth Forum in Tokyo.
"The existing release, they have to implement that first before doing anything more," said Sadamori, who is currently research director at the Canon Institute for Global Studies.
His comments come after the G7 set a four-month deadline for its member states to release the last 100 million barrels of oil stocks they committed to in March after collectively pledging to refrain from energy export restrictions within the organization.
The IEA, which counts all the G7 nations among its 32 member states, committed to releasing 400 million barrels of oil to the market in March, but has so far only recorded 300 million barrels of deliveries, according to data published in August. Of the supplies released to the market, 110 million barrels came from the Americas, 112 million barrels from Asia-Oceania and 68 million barrels from Europe, the IEA reported in August.
"So far, despite the massive scale of disruption of the Strait of Hormuz ... global oil markets somehow managed the situation," Sadamori said.
"This is thanks to a few factors. First, we had plenty of stocks before the crisis happened as the oil market was enjoying a very well-supplied market before the crisis," Sadamori said as he referred to "a massive surge" in exports from the Western Hemisphere, the US, Brazil and others outside the Persian Gulf region.
In addition to the IEA's oil release, Sadamori said China had reduced its oil imports "quite dramatically" since the start of the war in the Middle East.
On the G7's four-month deadline for the release of 100 million barrels of oil products, Sadamori said, "I think that the particular focus will be paid to the products like the diesel and jet fuels."
"When we look at the implementation of the stock release actions by the regions, the Asia Pacific has already implemented all of their stock release quotas. Indeed, they have exceeded the quotas because Japan released a lot more than agreed in the stock release actions agreed on March 11," Sadamori said.

"North America released about three-quarters, while in the case of Europe, they released just a little more than 60% of the agreed amount," he said. "So in that respect, I think it is really important for them to complete their stock release as agreed."
LNG outlook Commenting on the winter LNG supply and demand outlook,
Sadamori said, "The outlook is not so hard compared with the case of oil," citing newly commissioned supply capacity in North America, including Canada, despite Middle East supply disruptions being "quite substantial."
"But in the case of natural gas, for the power generation fuels, there are some countries that are switching to other sources like coal for the power generation. So that's also easing the tightness of the market," Sadamori said.
"Having said that, whether we can survive the winter would depend on various factors," including a mild winter in Europe.
The low filing of underground gas storage in Europe remains "a bit of a worrisome factor," Sadamori said.
EU-wide gas storage was 72.97% full as of Oct. 7, according to the latest data from Gas Infrastructure Europe.
"And I understand that they are going to discuss how they would handle the current policy to completely phase out Russian LNG imports," he said.
"So I think we need to see how European countries will discuss whether to postpone it or do it as agreed."
The EU is set to ban all imports of Russian LNG from Jan. 1, 2027, under legislation approved at the start of this year.
Some European companies with long-term Russian LNG contracts can continue to divert some shipments outside the EU until at least late July 2027 under a carve-out in the EU's latest sanctions package against Moscow, approved in July.
"We're not sure whether they will implement this Russian LNG phaseout or not," Sadamori said. "We need to see how they decide in the remaining months of this year... We'll see what European policymakers decide."
-- Takeo Kumagai
Syria oil pipeline attacks continue amid domestic fuel shortage
Third pipeline incident in 10 days
Crude imports drop to zero in recent months
Shortages trigger protests across country
A Syrian crude pipeline was damaged in an attack Oct. 7, in the third incident at oil and gas facilities in the country in around a week, the Syrian Petroleum Company said in a statement the same day.
Repair works are underway on the pipeline in the Al-Hasakah area in Syria's northeast, the statement said.
"This attack comes in the context of a series of attacks that have occurred in recent times on a number of oil [pipe]lines and facilities in Syria," the statement said.

In the nearby Deir ez-Zor province, a fire broke out on a gas pipeline in the Shula area Sept. 29, knocking some power stations out of service, while on Oct. 1, an explosion at the Tishreen pressure reduction station damaged a pipeline, although pumping resumed the next day, according to statements from SPC.
In mid-August, there was an explosion on the Jabsa gas export line that halted gas flows and affected electricity generation in an attack SPC at the time described as a "deliberate act of sabotage."
Syria is working to rebuild its oil and gas infrastructure and industry after a long civil war, but the country has faced ongoing security challenges, including from remaining pockets of Islamic State fighters. However, the militant group's activity has fallen to record low levels, according to a September analysis from the Middle East Institute.
Local regional media reported that suspects have been arrested for the latest attack, though neither the perpetrators nor a motive have been identified.
The attacks on oil and gas infrastructure have coincided with widespread diesel shortages and a mid-September price hike that helped spur protests amid a backlash over the cost of living.
The energy ministry said at the time that the price increases were temporary and reflected higher procurement costs and Syria's dependence on imported supplies. Since the ouster of former President Bashar al-Assad at the end of 2024, Syria has relied primarily on Russia for crude imports and a few scattered donations from other nations, including Saudi Arabia.
Syria imported 35,000-90,000 barrels/day of crude each month throughout most of 2026, but no imports have been recorded in August, September, or so far in October, according to S&P Global Commodities At Sea. Syria has two refineries at which crude is processed into products that typically serve the domestic market.
The country imported 43,000 b/d of gasoline and diesel in September, up from an average of around 25,000 b/d throughout much of 2026, CAS data showed.
Syria's domestic crude production has climbed over the last year, but still remains below required levels to meet domestic demand.

Philippine energy secretary unveils oil reserve, fuel transition plans
Philippines targets 15%-35% oil cut by 2050
1 mil barrel oil reserve facility due Dec 2026
Philippine Energy Secretary Sharon Garin unveiled two landmark energy policy frameworks on Oct. 8, outlining a roadmap to reduce the country's heavy dependence on imported oil while accelerating the buildout of renewable energy.
Speaking at the Philippine Energy Investment Forum in Manila, held alongside the ASEAN Energy Business Forum, Garin formally

presented the 2026 National Oil and Gas Co-Investment Plan and the fuel transition plan, which together set out a coordinated strategy for managing fuel supply under current conditions while progressively reducing the Philippines' exposure to global oil market disruptions.
The announcements carry direct implications for crude and refined products trade flows into the Philippines, which relies on the Middle East for around 90% of its crude oil imports and where oil accounts for 47.7% of final energy consumption, Garin said.
Energy security The urgency behind the new policy frameworks was
underscored by the energy emergency declared earlier this year, Garin said. On March 24, President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. issued Executive Order 110, declaring a national energy emergency in response to a sharp rise in global oil prices linked to the conflict in the Middle East.
The Department of Energy said it maintained domestic fuel stocks at 50 days of supply throughout the crisis, which is above the regulatory minimum of 30 days, and implemented weekly supply adjustments to prevent hoarding and distribution disruptions. A temporary suspension of the wholesale electricity spot market was also credited with averting a 7- to 12 Philippinepeso increase in consumer electricity bills.
The NOGCP establishes a systematic framework for managing fuel investment priorities and government response protocols during supply disruptions, detailing the roles of the DOE, the Department of Transportation, and local government units, Garin said.
The fuel transition plan sets out sector-by-sector pathways to reduce oil dependence, with a baseline scenario targeting a 15% reduction by 2030 and 35% by 2050, she added.
Under a more aggressive scenario, the Philippines is targeting a 30% reduction by 2030, 50% by 2045, and more than half by 2050, according to the DOE.
Transport, which accounts for 70% of domestic oil demand, is identified as the primary lever for reducing consumption, Garin said.
The government is targeting electric vehicles to comprise 25% of the national vehicle fleet by 2030 and 80% by 2040, supported by at least 7,000 electric vehicle charging stations nationwide by 2028, Garin said. The fuel transition plan also targets a 50% sustainable aviation fuel blend and a 30% alternative marine fuel blend by 2050.
To bolster physical supply security, the DOE said it is advancing the Philippine Strategic Petroleum Reserve under the Philippine National Oil Corp.
An initial stockpiling facility targeting at least 1 million barrels is scheduled for completion by the end of December 2026, with a longer-term target of 15 million barrels. Proposed locations include Bataan and Palawan, with the government seeking joint venture partners or independent investors for the expanded infrastructure, including oil storage tanks and potential refinery

reached 20.1 GW, while installed capacity stood at 32.5 GW as of July 2026. Electricity demand is growing at 6 to 7% annually, and as high as 14% in some urban centers. Net energy imports currently account for 51.5% of the total primary energy supply.
The government has also committed to a renewable energy share of 35% of the generation mix by 2030, 50% by 2040, and more than 50 percent by 2050.

Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
Pertamina issues tender seeking crude to Balikpapan
BSP sells Dec-loading Champion, Seria Light cargoes
Limited arbitrage Brazilian crude trades toward Asia: trader
The Asia-Pacific sweet crude market on Oct. 8 saw the emergence of Pertamina's buy tender along with initial results of Brunei Shell Petroleum's tender, while some trade source noted that arbitrage Brazilian crude movements remained limited.
Indonesian refiner Pertamina was seen seeking various crude grades for delivery in early December, to their refinery in Balikpapan, according to a tender document seen by Platts, along with two trade sources. The tender was set to close on Oct. 8, with validity till Oct. 10. However, Pertamina was heard to have later cancelled the tender, according to a few market sources.
On the medium, sweet crude front, Brunei Shell Petroleum is was heard to have sold a total of 600,000 barrels of Bruneian crude across two separate sell tenders, comprising a 300,000-barrel Champion Export cargo loading Dec. 25-26 and a 300,000-barrel Seria Light Export Blend cargo loading Dec. 27-28, to a refiner, according to sources with knowledge of the matter. Price details of the trade however, remain unascertained, with some traders noting that the bids for the cargoes have been rather strong.
Comparatively, Brunei Shell Petroleum had previously sold the Champion crude scheduled for loading over Oct. 24-25 to an oil major at a discount of about 50 cents/barrel against Malaysian crude oil LMKK prices, Platts reported earlier, citing sources with knowledge of the matter.
On the other hand, valuations for December-loading barrels of Malaysian key Labuan crude were heard to be lower on the month, hovering at premiums of $15/b to $16/b to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, FOB, a crude trader shared.
Over on the arbitrage crude front, a trader noted that little movement of January-arrival barrels of Brazilian Tupi crude eastwards were seen.
Upstream crude oil futures meanwhile, saw prices leaping as more ships were attacked in the Middle East in recent days, Ukraine struck another Russian refinery and a hurricane forced US producers to shut some of their production in the Gulf of Mexico.
Elsewhere, South Korea and Egypt are seeking closer

cooperation in upstream oil development, energy infrastructure and Suez-linked logistics, as security risks in the Red Sea complicate South Korean refiners' Middle Eastern crude shipments, according to the Korea Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the trade ministry and Korea National Oil Corp. Oct. 7.

Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
Activity tapers off with October placed, November uncertain
Punitive freight costs remain key indicator for cycle ahead
North Sea sweet crude values held steady Oct. 8, with October-loaders expected to have found homes, while traders continued to eye developments in adjacent regions for cues on upcoming supply balances.
Bidding activity across the Platts Market on Close assessment process had petered out on the day, with "October pretty much all placed," said a Europe-based North Sea crude trader. "November is a question mark though; there are too many moving parts."
Expensive freight rates across the Atlantic basin remained a key concern, with traders seeing impacts from both WTI Midland and the distribution of releases from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve announced Sept. 29.
"The US SPR tender is ongoing still, but I think given it's sour and freight is prohibitively expensive, it's likely to be unawarded or to stay domestic," said a second trader. "[However], the SPR barrels are all 620,000-680,000 barrel parcels on Aframaxes, so if it does get exported at all, it will be more for Europe."
A third trader said: "I think the big question is whether barrels can move here cheap enough."
While surging freight costs seemed to have tempered an anticipated lengthening of European sour crude balances following the US SPR release, the sour segment continued to see differentials for Norway's Johan Sverdrup weakening on the day.
The Oct. 8 MOC session saw BP selling a Johan Sverdrup FOB Mongstad cargo loading over Oct. 23-25 to Equinor at a $2.70/b premium over Dated Brent, having demonstrated value lower throughout the session.
Traders also noted a steeply backwardated market structure disproportionately pressuring crude differentials on the prompt, with values for November-loading Johan Sverdrup cargoes pegged above the $10/b mark during the day.
In public chaining activity, a Forties cargo loading across Nov. 29 to Dec. 1 associated with the parcel number F1108 was seen publicly running in chains during afternoon trading and last seen with Trafigura.
With over 20 days before the cargo's final day of chaining eligibility, the nomination comes relatively early compared to recent cycles -- eliciting some surprise from market participants.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Natasha Tan
Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
Sellers struggle to attract bids in bearish FOB market
Spot trading thin with uncertain freight economics
West African crude sale tenders struggled to attract bids from prospective buyers, trading sources said Oct. 8, with elevated

freight rates continuing to bite sentiment. Platts assessed the signature West Africa-UK Continent
130,000-mt fixture at w975 on the day, higher by w50 from Oct. 7. Nigerian crude, which prices free-on-board meaning buyers bear the cost of shipping, continued to feel downward pressure.
"It's hard to really say where the bottom is, it's a horrendous market to try and trade. Guyana has corrected a lot and Brazilian [delivered] offers are absorbing all the freight, so it makes it difficult for anyone to pass on freight costs to buyers," one WAF trader said Oct. 8.
Buyers were heard to be few and far between in the Nigerian market. A second trader said that the past few days were the first time they had ever seen no bids for Nigerian sell tenders.
"The situation is very complicated, and I haven't seen freight rates like this in my life," the second trader said. "I will say around 90% of Nigerian [November program] is unsold as people wait to see negative differentials for grades like Forcados and Bonga."
Platts assessed the two grades lower by 50 cents/barrel day over day Oct. 8 to $2.55/b and $2.75/b premiums, respectively, to global benchmark Dated Brent.
With Eastern arbitrage opportunities narrowing, sources said spot activity remained mostly at a standstill.
"Sellers are taking all the pain while freight rates take all the gain," a third trader said. "It's risky to buy anything until you have a ship, as freight could easily add a couple of million dollars to your costs. Unless you have time-chartered vessels, you are better off waiting a bit."
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- George Delaney
Americas Crude
Appetite for US crude oil exports climbs despite high freight
South Korea tops US crude destinations at 568,600 b/d
VLCC rates to Asia double, closing arbitrage window
European refining margins widen gap over Singapore
US crude exports remained elevated at 3.5 million b/d in September, edging up from 3.4 million b/d in August, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, but well below the May peak of 4.4 million b/d. South Korea was the largest single destination at 568,600 b/d, followed by the Netherlands at 459,000 b/d, with Japan, France, Canada and the UK also among the top receivers.
Volumes into Asia climbed across the region, including Northeast Asia, where US exports rose from 793,000 b/d in August to 1.1 million b/d. Exports into Southeast Asia climbed from 292,000 b/d to 528,000 b/d, while volumes into the Indian subcontinent climbed from 68,000 b/d to 220,000 b/d over the same period.
Asian volumes in September largely reflect cargoes fixed before VLCC freight on USGC-Asia routes roughly doubled.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

The Hormuz disruption initially drove Asian refiners toward US crude as an alternative supply source, but its knock-on effect on tanker markets has since eroded the economics of those trade flows.
Fixtures to Asia, including some UKC options, climbed to 7.8 million metric tons in September compared to 4.8 million in August.
Surging tanker rates have effectively closed the spot eastbound arbitrage for US crude exports to Asia. Arbitrage calculations favor WTI Midland barrels going to Northwest Europe, where refining margins have strengthened and the freight burden remains comparatively manageable.
The arbitrage for WTI MEH into Rotterdam was $19.99/b as of Oct. 8, while the equivalent incentive into Singapore had fallen to minus $17.01/b and into Northeast Asia at minus $19.77/b, according to S&P Global Energy CERA.
VLCC rates on the USGC-China route surged from an average of $79.21/mt in August to a $156.19/mt average in September, while the USGC-Singapore route averages rose from $75.51/ mt to $152.49/mt over the same period, according to Platts assessments.
The USGC-UK Continent and Mediterranean route also roughly doubled, climbing from $40.26/mt to $80.82/mt. While the percentage increases were broadly similar across all three routes, approximately 97% to 102%, the economic impact is far more severe on Asian voyages because of the greater distance involved, higher bunker consumption and the longer period a vessel is tied up in transit.
South Korean and Japanese refiners, in particular, may continue to lift WTI cargoes under term supply arrangements or for crude diversification, regardless of whether the prompt spot arbitrage is open.
The Netherlands' 459,000 b/d intake is consistent with the strongly positive Rotterdam arbitrage and reflects the country's dual role as both a refining center and a transshipment hub for the broader Northwest European market.
With the WTI MEH-versus-Forties incentive at $14.72/b, Europe is the economic clearing destination for incremental US barrels, and transatlantic flows are likely to remain supported even after the climb in freight costs.
Asia vs Europe The result is a widening of the refining economics gap
between the Atlantic Basin and Asia. Platts WTI MEH cracking margins into Northwest Europe climbed from $44.13/b in August to $47.47/b in September, and into the US Gulf Coast from $46.75/b to $50.12/b, according to CERA.
By contrast, the equivalent margin into Singapore fell from $29.13/b to $23.09/b over the same period.
All three margins remain dramatically stronger than February levels, when Singapore was at just $5.60/b, NWE at $8.62/b and the USGC at $10.21/b.
Midland's discount to WTI MEH, the Houston-area coastal marker that most directly captures export optionality, widened

from $0.30/b to $0.82/b, while its discount to WTI FOB USGC increased from $1.33/b to $1.83/b.
WTI Midland's premium to Cushing WTI widened from $0.68/b in August to $0.98/b in September.
The pattern shows that coastal, export-ready barrels attracted a growing premium over inland crude, reflecting competition between domestic refiners and Atlantic Basin exporters for Gulf Coast supply.
-- Binish Azhar
US Gulf producers shut in 1.283 million b/d, 1.13 Bcf/d ahead of Hurricane Isaias
Chevron shuts in five facilities
Occidental monitoring situation
121 manned platforms evacuated
Producers in the Gulf of Mexico have shut in 1.283 million barrels/day of oil production and 1.13 billion cubic feet/day of natural gas output ahead of Hurricane Isaias, the Marine Minerals Administration said Oct. 8.
The shut-in volumes represent nearly 63% of the current daily oil production and 57% of the natural gas production in the Gulf of Mexico, MMA said in its latest update at 2 pm ET.
The curtailed volumes increased from 511,619 b/d and 350 MMcf/d on Oct. 7, MMA said at the time.
"Based on data from offshore operator reports submitted as of 11 am CT today [Oct. 8], personnel have been evacuated from a total of 121 production platforms, 32.61% of the 371 manned platforms" in the Gulf, it said, adding that personnel have been evacuated from five non-dynamically positioned rigs in operation.
A total of four of the 17 dynamically positioned rigs have moved off location out of the storm's path as a precaution, MMA said.
Chevron said Oct. 8 it has shut in production at five offshore facilities in preparation for Hurricane Isaias. While a majority of the company's offshore personnel are being transported onshore, production continues at its other four platforms, Chevron said.
Shell said Oct. 7 that it was shutting in production and evacuating staff from its Mars, Olympus, Ursa, Vito, and Appomattox assets as a cautionary measure.
Occidental Petroleum Corp. said Oct. 8 it was closely monitoring the current weather conditions. "Our offshore facilities maintain established severe-weather preparedness plans, and we have begun implementing these procedures to protect the safety of our people and the environment," Occidental said.
Hurricane Isaias is a Category 1 hurricane with maximum sustained winds near 85miles/hour, located about 185 miles north of Progreso, Mexico, the National Hurricane Center said in its latest update.
-- Ashok Dutta

October 8, 2026

Argentina's Santa Cruz says talks advance with Pan American on D-129 shale exploration
Pan American is testing D-129 in Chubut
BP-backed firm looking for future growth
Crown Point also plans to drill in the play
Santa Cruz, Argentina's third-largest oil province, is in talks with Pan American Energy to explore the D-129 shale play within its territory, building on the oil producer's testing of the play in neighboring Chubut province, Energy Minister Jamie Álvarez said.
"The agreement with Pan American Energy is 60% complete," he said in a late Oct. 7 statement. "This marks the beginning of a new phase for Santa Cruz."
Pan American, a joint venture of Argentina's Bridas and UKbased BP, began testing D-129 in Chubut in southern Patagonia earlier this year. The company has said exploration of the play is part of a long-term strategy to extend its production growth beyond Vaca Muerta, the huge shale play in northern Patagonia where it is the fifth-largest oil and gas producer.
Álvarez said an announcement on the project could come "in a week or two," adding that D-129 could be a new source of oil production growth in the province after 80 years of developing heavy conventional reserves that are now maturing.
"This opens a very important door to the future in terms of unconventional development," he said.
He added that Canada-based Crown Point Energy plans to drill three wells in D-129, and that there is also potential in the Palermo Aike shale play to the south of the province and in Neocomiano in the north, near D-129.
For Santa Cruz, he said the goal of these shale investments is to reverse the decline in production since 2017, helping sustain a key industry in the province. The province's crude output fell 46% to 53,000 b/d in August from an average of 98,000 b/d in 2017, according to data from the national Energy Secretariat.
-- Charles Newbery
Brazil's Brava Energia boosts September production on new oil well, Potiguar restarts
Papa Terra PPT-52 pumps first oil
Expects additional well in fourth quarter
Potiguar gains momentum
Brazilian independent oil and natural gas producer Brava Energia increased output in September after completing maintenance work at the offshore Papa Terra field, pumping first oil from the new PPT-52 well and continuing to restart onshore wells in the Potiguar Basin, according to the company's latest production report.
Brava Energia pumped an average of 82,961 barrels of oil equivalent a day in September, up 1.7% from 81,608 boe/d in August, the company said after markets closed Oct. 7.

September's production, however, was down 9.7% from 91.833 boe/d in the same month a year ago.
In the third quarter, Brava Energia produced 82,387 boe/d, the company said.
September's production gains were led by the Papa Terra field, which rebounded from a maintenance shutdown carried out at the field July 29-Aug. 7, Brava Energia said. In addition, Brava Energia pumped its first oil from the new PPT-52 production well Sept. 29. The company hasn't yet provided any details on oil flows, noting that production from the well was still being stabilized after the start-up.
The PPT-52 production well was part of a four-well drilling campaign at the offshore Papa Terra and Atlanta fields, which began in March. In addition to the PPT-52 production well, Brava Energia is also drilling the PPT-53 production well at Papa Terra, as well as the ATL-9H and ATL-10H production wells at Atlanta.
Brava Energia also expects to begin production from the PPT-53 well before the end of 2026, according to the company. The two wells were expected to produce about 11,000-12,000 b/d combined. A pipe-laying supply vessel (PLSV) has been at Papa Terra since July, connecting the wells to the 3R floating production, storage, and offloading vessel and a tension-leg wellhead platform installed at the field.
Papa Terra produced 19,415 barrels/day of oil and 430 boe/d of natural gas in September, up from 19,250 b/d and 299 boe/d in August, Brava Energia said. September's oil and gas output also advanced from 11,654 b/d and 482 boe/d in September 2025.
In Q3, Papa Terra's output was 19,245 b/d and 375 boe/d, Brava Energia said.
Brava Energia holds a 62.5% operating stake in Papa Terra, which is expected to rise to 100% in the second half of 2026 after the final decision in an ongoing arbitration case against erstwhile partner Nova Tecnica Energy is published later this year, according to company officials.
Output from the Atlanta field, meanwhile, retreated slightly month over month in September amid ongoing natural declines, the report showed.
Atlanta produced 23,778 b/d and 1,145 boe/d in September, down from 24,366 b/d and 1,168 boe/d in August, Brava Energia said. September's output also fell from 27,659 b/d and 1,259 boe/d in September 2025. In Q3, Atlanta produced 24,362 b/d and 1,168 boe/d.
Brava Energia expects to return Atlanta to its previous production levels in Q2 2027, when the two new production wells are expected to start operations. The drilling campaign at Atlanta is scheduled to start in October, when Brava Energia plans to relocate the Constellation-operated Lone Star drilling rig to the field. The ATL-9H and ATL-10H production wells were estimated to pump about 15,000-20,000 b/d combined.
Brava Energia holds an 80% operating stake in the Atlanta field, while Westlawn Americas Offshore retains the remaining 20% equity stake.

Nigeria to cap retail fuel prices to tackle rising costs
Government negotiating price cap for retail gasoline
NNPC offers 30-day discount at service stations
Opposition parties call for return to fuel subsidy

The Nigerian government is taking measures to cap gasoline prices that retailers can charge at the country's fuel stations in a bid to combat soaring global prices linked to the Middle East conflict, its finance minister announced in a press briefing on Oct. 8.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

14

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

Responding to ongoing volatility putting pressure on the Nigerian consumer, the government is negotiating a new price ceiling with marketers and retailers, which would be set at 1,350 nairas/ liter ($1.02/l), Finance Minister Taiwo Oyedele told reporters in Abuja.
"Pump prices do not have to follow every swing in global crude prices or the exchange rate," the minister said, adding that refiners and importers should absorb any difference between the price cap and prevailing market rates.
In addition to the proposed cap, retail stations managed by the Nigerian National Petroleum Corp. are additionally offering a 30-day discount on gasoline supplies, with priority for public transport operators, the minister said.
He separated the proposed interventions from the fuel subsidies historically offered by the Nigerian government, which were abolished as part of sweeping economic reforms introduced by President Bola Tinubu when he took office in 2023.
"This is neither a subsidy nor a price control; it is designed to smooth prices over time, rather than suppressing them," Oyedele said. "Returning to subsidy will make matters worse, not better," he said.
In 2022, when the outbreak of the Russia-Ukraine war sent oil prices soaring, the scheme cost Nigeria roughly $10 billion, the International Monetary Fund reported, warning that the policy needed to be scrapped to manage the country's debt.
In the energy sector, subsidies also contributed to smuggling across Nigeria's borders, inflating the country's import volumes at a time when it relied almost exclusively on fuel produced abroad.
Other support options Oyedele acknowledged the impact of cutting the subsidy on
the country's population -- which has recently consumed around 260,000 b/d of gasoline for use in the transportation sector as well as for backup generators. Gasoline is currently retailing at around 1,400 nairas/l ($1.05/l), he said, compared with levels of under 200 nairas/l when subsidies were still in place in early 2023.
According to data from Nigeria's downstream oil regulator, the Nigerian Midstream and Downstream Petroleum Regulatory Authority, demand has already come under pressure as prices have risen, taking national consumption from 60.2 million l/d in January to 48.3 million l/d in August.
Oyedele said the government plans to forward sell crude oil to domestic refiners as part of its proposed measures to help support the market, without providing specifics on the agreed threshold.
NNPC already has an agreement with the country's only commercial-scale refinery, Dangote, to supply it with crude oil in the country's local currency, and has a commitment to offer it roughly 350,000 b/d of oil under the country's domestic crude supply obligation.
The government's new proposals follow mounting public discontent over the gasoline price, which has sparked recent threats of mass strikes from the Nigerian Labor Congress union. With a national election coming up in 2027, challengers

to Tinubu's All Progressives Congress have vowed to restore the gasoline subsidy, including former Vice President Atiku Abubakar and Peter Obi, who is running for the Nigeria Democratic Congress.
-- Kelly Norways, Newsdesk-Nigeria
Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
US gasoline stocks rise to 204.744 M barrels

The Asian gasoline complex was expected to be rangebound to softer Oct. 8, as market participants continued to monitor Chinese gasoline exports and tracking a narrowing US RBOBBrent crack.
The US RBOB-Brent crack was pegged at $34.67/b at 0300 GMT Oct. 8, narrowing from the Platts assessment at $39.42/b at the Asian close Oct. 7.
The US RBOB-Brent crack narrowed amid a rise in total US gasoline stocks to 204.744 million barrels in the week to Oct. 2, from 204.362 million barrels the week before, the US Energy Information Administration data showed.
However, Midwestern gasoline inventories set a fresh all-time low in the week ended Oct. 2, with total gasoline stocks in PADD 2 falling by 420,000 barrels to 41.41 million barrels, the lowest level recorded since the EIA began publishing the data series in 1990, Platts previously reported.
Total US gasoline supplied, a proxy for demand, rose to 8.769 million b/d in the same week from 8.689 million b/d the week before, according to the EIA data.
In tender news, South Korea's GS Caltex was heard offering 600,000 barrels of 92 RON gasoline for Nov. 14-18 and Nov. 29Dec. 3 loading, via a tender that closes Oct. 8, Platts previously reported.

Jet
European jet fuel market expected to tighten with dipping imports
US, Asia exports decrease in October
Jet vs ULSD regrade hits 5-month high
The European jet fuel market is expected to tighten over the next few weeks as flows from Asia drop due to Chinese export restrictions, those from the US drop due to low stocks and as local refineries have prioritized diesel production over the last few months.
Refineries have been prioritizing diesel output in the wake of the Russian export ban.
Platts assessed the regrade, the spread between the European jet barge crack and diesel barge crack at minus $11.94/b on Aug. 21. A negative regrade incentivizes refineries to produce more diesel fuel over its co-distillate jet.

Now, after two months of refiners prioritizing diesel output, the jet fuel market has tightened, resulting in the regrade flipping to positive.
The regrade hit a 5-month high of $16.45/b on Oct. 7, the highest level since April 28.
"Jet is stronger than diesel now because refineries were max diesel for a while, volumes from the US have dipped and Chinese export restrictions mean East of Suez exports are also not increasing," said a European distillates trader, adding that diesel has seen some alleviation of tightness from strategic stock releases.
Europe's imports of jet fuel from the US hit 42,000 mt in October so far, down from 248,000 mt last month, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. About 731,000 mt of jet fuel was exported from the US to Europe in August.
US exports of gasoline and jet fuel slipped for the week ended Oct. 2, while diesel exports continued to climb, according to data released Oct. 7 from the US Energy Information Administration.
China suspends exports Chinese refiners have halted clean oil product exports and the
suspension could extend into November amid lower refinery runs. "The regrade in Singapore is very strong and the jet arb from
Asia is not viable - could be because of the Chinese ban," said a second Europe-based trader.
South Korea has exported 491,000 mt of jet fuel to Europe so far this month, down from the record highs of 571,000 mt in September, according to CAS data. China has exported just 14,000 mt to Europe in October, about half the volumes seen last month.
However, other Asia-based traders said that selling products to Europe remains favorable given high flat prices, despite high freight costs, and they will continue to monitor Chinese export quotas.
"Exporting a lot of volumes to Europe now - there are great margins," they said. "So, we will probably continue like this."
"We need more production in trans-Atlantic. So now it's pricing for that switch back to jet for refineries," the first European trader added.
As headline-driven volatility continues, flat prices remain elevated at more than double the same time last year. Platts assessed the CIF jet NWE cargo price at $1,611.75/mt on Oct. 7, while it was assessed at $709.75/mt, a year ago

The spokesperson added that timeline details of construction and commission would be shared once available.

The NSW government's Investment Delivery Authority endorsed the expansion as one of five fuel security projects announced on Sept. 20. The projects will receive non-financial support to resolve delivery barriers, coordinate government agencies and accelerate development, according to the government's announcement.
Vopak's existing Port Botany terminal imports and distributes petroleum products to Greater Sydney and regional NSW. It already has a jet fuel pipeline connection to Sydney Airport, as well as metropolitan pipeline connections for gasoline and diesel, according to the company's website.
The facility has two vessel berths and truck-loading infrastructure and handles petroleum products, biodiesel, ethanol, and bitumen. Its services include blending and additive injection, while its certifications include ISCC EU, ISCC Plus and ISCC CORSIA.
Expanding the independent, open-access facility would strengthen fuel security and competition, while its SAF storage capability would support Australia's energy transition, Vopak Terminals Australia managing director Mats de Groot said.
The Australian government has accelerated support for low-carbon liquid fuels, with A$1.1 billion ($764.5 million) to be delivered under the Cleaner Fuels Program, announced as part of its 2026-27 federal budget, announced in May.

Costs, feedstocks delay global SAF scale-up to 2040s: DNV
SAF costs remain triple fossil jet prices
Biomass feedstock caps fuel at 10% supply
China emerges as major SAF production hub
Sustainable aviation fuel will not begin materially displacing fossil jet fuel until the 2040s as high costs, limited binding offtake and biomass constraints slow production growth, DNV said in its Energy Transition Outlook 2026, published Oct. 8.
Aviation energy demand is forecast to rise 77% from current levels to 26 EJ/year, equivalent to about 13 million barrels/day of jet fuel, by 2060 as global air-travel demand doubles, DNV said. Despite growing SAF use, oil will supply about as much aviation energy in 2060 as it does today.
"SAF will not start to displace fossil oil before the 2040s," the report said.
Current bio-SAF is about three times more expensive than fossil jet fuel, while synthetic e-SAF costs about 10 times more, DNV said, citing 2026 European reference-price data.
Production growth has also been constrained by a lack of binding offtake agreements between SAF producers and airlines. Stronger measures supporting production or enforcing demand will be required because SAF is expected to retain a premium over conventional jet fuel during the forecast period.
Constraints along biomass pathway Bio-based SAF is forecast to dominate aviation's initial
transition in the 2030s, before hydrogen-derived synthetic fuels enter the mix in greater volumes from the 2040s as production costs decline.
However, the main commercial pathway, hydroprocessed esters and fatty acids, faces feedstock availability sufficient to cover only around 10% of aviation fuel demand, DNV said.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

17

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

Other approved production pathways face similar constraints, meaning sustainably available biomass will be insufficient to meet total aviation demand even as additional technologies receive regulatory approval.
Biomass-based SAF demand is forecast to reach 7 EJ/year in 2060, DNV said. Conventional jet fuel will still supply 16 EJ/year, or 59% of total aviation energy consumption, compared with 1% bio-SAF use currently.
The long-term feedstock ceiling will create demand for more expensive but less biomass-constrained e-SAF, produced using renewable hydrogen and a carbon source.
Yet DNV has reduced its broader hydrogen and derivatives forecast, with high costs and constrained policy support slowing projects. Its 2060 estimate for hydrogen use in aviation and maritime transportation is 8 million mt/year below the forecast published in its May hydrogen outlook.
Regional policy divergence Europe will remain the leading demand-driven SAF market,
supported by the ReFuelEU Aviation mandate, which requires the fuel's share to rise from 2% in 2025 to 70% in 2050, DNV said.
However, DNV added the 2050 requirement, including its 35% synthetic-fuel component, was unlikely to be met. Europe will likely need to import SAF from regions with stronger production expansion policies, particularly from the Greater China region.
Greater China is initially scaling HEFA output using used cooking oil, but also plans additional biofuel and e-SAF pathways. Domestic aviation demand remains voluntary, with much of its current output exported to Europe.
If China introduces the domestic blending requirement expected by DNV, local consumption could expand rapidly, reducing volumes available to overseas buyers.
North America, meanwhile, aims to use its agricultural and forestry base to meet demand through domestic bio-SAF production. Current capacity is about 2% of regional jet fuel demand, while said US and Canadian goals for 2030 together represent roughly 9%.
However, the downward revision of the US Clean Fuel Production Credit will constrain near-term SAF output growth, DNV said.

Platts European Jet Daily Market Analysis
Jet flat prices strengthen on day
Regrade strengthens as inflows dip
European jet fuel differentials rose Oct. 8 as the market is expecting tightness with dipping East of Suez and US exports, according to market participants.
Platts assessed the Jet CIF NWE cargo price up $67.75 on the day to a premium of $1,679.50/mt. The Platts Jet FOB FARAG barge price was also up $67.75 on the day to a premium of $1,689.25/mt.
The European jet fuel market is expected to tighten over the next few weeks as flows from Asia drop due to Chinese export restrictions, those from the US drop due to low stocks and as local refineries have prioritized diesel production over the last few months.
Refineries have been prioritizing diesel output in the wake of the Russian export ban.
Platts assessed the regrade, the spread between the European jet barge crack and diesel barge crack at minus $11.94/b on Aug. 21. A negative regrade incentivizes refineries to produce more diesel fuel over its co-distillate jet.
Now, after two months of refiners prioritizing diesel output, the jet fuel market has tightened, resulting in the regrade flipping to positive.
The regrade hit a five-month high of $16.45/b on Oct. 7, the highest level since April 28. It was at $15.30/b on Oct. 8.
"Jet is stronger than diesel now because refineries were max diesel for a while, volumes from the US have dipped, and Chinese export restrictions mean East of Suez exports are also not increasing," said a European distillates trader, adding that diesel has seen some alleviation of tightness from strategic stock releases.
Europe's imports of jet fuel from the US hit 42,000 mt in October so far, down from 248,000 mt last month, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. About 731,000 mt of jet fuel was exported from the US to Europe in August.

US exports of gasoline and jet fuel slipped for the week ended Oct. 2, while diesel exports continued to climb, according to data released Oct. 7 from the US Energy Information Administration.
Chinese refiners have halted clean oil product exports and the suspension could extend into November amid lower refinery runs.
"The regrade in Singapore is very strong and the jet arb from Asia is not viable - could be because of the Chinese ban," said a second Europe-based trader.
South Korea has exported 491,000 mt of jet fuel to Europe so far this month, down from the record highs of 571,000 mt in September, according to CAS data. China has exported just 14,000 mt to Europe in October, about half the volumes seen last month.
However, other Asia-based traders said that selling products to Europe remains favorable given high flat prices, despite high freight costs, and they will continue to monitor Chinese export quotas.
"Exporting a lot of volumes to Europe now - there are great margins," they said. "So, we will probably continue like this."
"We need more production in transatlantic. So now it's pricing for that switch back to jet for refineries," the first European trader added.

Diesel
High European diesel prices stifle demand: sources
Prices jump amid reduced energy security
UK diesel pump prices hit record 119.52 pence/liter
The sustained high price environment in Europe is diminishing buying interest for diesel at the wholesale and retail level, according to market participants.
On Sept. 15, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 10 ppm diesel CIF NWE cargo price at an all-time high of $1,664.75/ metric ton. Since then, the high cost of diesel has trickled down to the pump, sending retail prices surging and hampering end consumption.
In the week ended Oct. 5, UK diesel pump prices rose to their highest recorded level at 119.52 pence/liter, according to the latest data from the UK Department of Energy Security and Net Zero. This marks an increase of 56.6 pence/liter from the same week last year.
The rest of Europe is experiencing similar peaks, with pump prices averaging about 2.15/liter across all EU countries in the week ended Oct. 5, down 9 cents/l from the all-time high of 2.24/liter the week prior, according to data from the European Commission published Oct. 8.
"Consumer behavior is changing [with high retail prices]," said one European diesel trader. "People are filling up less, taking fewer journeys or looking for alternative transport. This is happening across Europe."

France to release 10 million barrels of diesel from emergency reserves: Lecornu
March commitment was 14.6 million barrels
Release aims to cut pump prices 0.12-0.18
IEA committed to release 100 million barrels
France has agreed to release 10 million barrels of diesel from its emergency reserves to try and curb rising fuel prices after a G7 commitment to draw down more stocks, its Prime Minister Sebastien Lecornu announced in a national address Oct. 7.
"Last Friday, President Macron convened an emergency meeting of the G7, which decided to mobilize strategic reserves in a coordinated manner. As part of this effort, we will make 10 million barrels of diesel from our reserves available," Lecornu told reporters, saying the decision could shave prices at the pump by around 0.12 to 0.18 per liter.
His comments follow a pledge from the International Energy Agency to try and accelerate the pace of its 400 million barrel stock release, which was announced in March but still has 100 million barrels left to complete. After mounting pressure from the US for other countries to release stocks, the G7 said its member states would prioritize getting diesel to the market within 20 days.
As part of the collective action in March, France committed to release 14.6 million barrels of oil, without specifying whether those volumes would be crude or oil products. In a Oct. 7 note, analysts at US investment bank JP Morgan estimated that France had already released 3 million barrels of diesel, and still has around 61 million barrels in reserve.
Together with Germany, the country is home to one of the largest stockpiles of diesel. According to reporting from Politico,

the country was directly approached by the US administration last week urging for it to release stocks, as the White House threatened the prospect of a potential diesel export ban in effort to cut domestic prices.
In an interview with Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Keisuke Sadamori, the IEA's former director of energy markets and security, observed that Asia Pacific was the only region to fully implement its IEA stock release commitments, and even overfulfill them thanks to extra injections from Japan.
"North America released about three-quarters, while in the case of Europe, they released just a little more than 60% of the agreed amount," he said. "So, in that respect, I think it is really important for them to complete their stock release as agreed."
As a gesture of goodwill to the government and consumers, the French oil company TotalEnergies has committed to cap prices for diesel and gasoline across its retail stations. The French government has additionally committed 1.4 billion in targeted relief to soften the impact of rising fuel costs on lowincome consumers, as well as key sectors like fishing, farming and construction, according to Budget Minister David Amiel.
According to new measures unveiled in September, the government committed to covering up to 70% of cost increases for the fishing sector after fishermen blockaded ports in the South of France to protest rising prices, while zero-interest loans are also being extended to keep supplies affordable.
However, costly subsidies have contributed to surging debt in the French economy, which currently faces a projected budget deficit of over 5% of GDP, one of the largest in the European Union, and borrowing costs hovering at a 24-year high.
France consumed roughly 750,000 b/d of diesel in 2025, and produced around 400,000 b/d domestically, according to estimates from S&P Global Energy CERA. In 2026, consumption is expected to contract by roughly 17,000 b/d -- or 2% -- according to the latest CERA view, consistent with a 2-5% annual consumption drop every year post-Covid.
-- Kelly Norways

-- Alexandra Vladimirova, David Neef
Diesel crunch deepens as shipping costs squeeze global oil trade
Refiners run 'max diesel' mode amid shortage: Phillips 66
Shipping costs to Asia add $40/barrel to crude price
Japan shifts one-third of imports to US oil

Crude flows from the Persian Gulf are recovering toward pre-disruption levels before the US-Iran war, but diesel and shipping markets remain under severe strain, prompting Asian and European buyers to seek more US oil and refined products, executives from Phillips 66 and Occidental Petroleum said Oct. 7.
Diesel remains acutely tight as the Middle East disruption coincides with reduced Russian refinery output and lower Chinese product exports, Mark Senn, Phillips 66 senior vice president of global trading and commercial, said at the Baker Institute's annual energy summit in Houston.
Ukrainian attacks have systematically targeted Russian refineries, curbing a major source of fuel exports, while China has reduced or at times halted product shipments, leaving US and European plants to cover more of the shortfall.
Phillips 66 has responded by running all its refineries in "max diesel mode," seeking to extract every additional barrel while operating safely, Senn said, as Gulf crude loadings were moving closer to pre-disruption levels, but lost refining capacity and export supply made diesel substantially harder to replace.
Tracking the crude recovery remained difficult because some tankers switched off transponders while transiting the Strait of Hormuz. Even so, crude availability was improving, whereas diesel balances remained the tightest part of the barrel, Senn said.
Oxy Senior Vice President of Energy Services Geoff Houlton cautioned that crude markets had not yet reached a comfortable position. Inventories had continued to decline in recent weeks, leaving the market exposed to further disruption even as Gulf loadings improved, he said.
OPEC remains a useful buffer, although it cannot impose its will on its largest members, former US Ambassador David Satterfield said during a separate panel at the summit. "Without OPEC, the degree of uncertainty, even in an admittedly uncertain market, would be much greater," he said.
Shipping has become another critical constraint. Houlton cited a Gulf Coast-to-Asia very large crude carrier fixture at about $80 million, equivalent to roughly $40/b. That would lift the delivered cost of crude priced near $90/b to about $130$140/b for an Asian buyer, illustrating how freight can erode the advantage of replacement supplies even when the oil itself is available.
Higher insurance costs, longer voyages and the use of tankers for short shuttle runs around the Gulf have reduced the vessels available for long-haul trade, the panelists said.
Satterfield said Iran had demonstrated that it could close the Strait of Hormuz, giving Tehran a significant form of power projection over global oil markets. He expected abnormal market and production conditions to remain the norm for about two years as Iran seeks to outlast US-led sanctions and the naval blockade while avoiding actions that would trigger a major military response.
Middle East barrels rerouted through the Red Sea can add about 20 days to voyages to Asia, Houlton said. Tonnage has also tightened sharply in the Aframax and Suezmax segments, making it increasingly difficult to split very large crude carrier cargoes,

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

21

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

according to shipping market sources. Charterers avoiding high VLCC rates would typically book
multiple Suezmaxes or Aframaxes instead, but the shortage of those smaller vessels is limiting that alternative. Asian refiners still need the crude and strong refining margins are supporting their willingness to secure replacement barrels despite elevated freight costs.
The disruption is also redirecting trade toward the US. Senn said Asian buyers were showing greater interest in LPG from Phillips 66's Gulf Coast system, while Asian and European refiners were approaching the company's Beaumont terminal for crude.
Houlton said Japan, which had sourced nearly all its crude from the Middle East before the shock, moved from taking almost no US crude to obtaining about one-third of its imports from the US over the summer--a rapid diversification by one of the world's most import-dependent markets.
Import-dependent governments are considering larger strategic stocks and a broader supplier base to reduce exposure to a single route or region, while Middle East producers may position inventories closer to consuming markets so that barrels remain accessible if another chokepoint closes. Rebuilding those stocks after the disruption could provide a tailwind for oil demand, Houlton said.

India's bunker fuel demand remained subdued in September and has continued to soften into October, as higher very low sulfur fuel oil prices relative to key regional hubs such as Singapore and Fujairah weighed on inquiries across major ports, market participants said.
"October has started on a dull note, with inquiry remaining very limited," a Dubai-based trader for the Indian market told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 8. "September was relatively quiet, with volumes lower across grades compared with previous months. The first half was moderately active, but weakened significantly in the second half."
"We were previously seeing around five to six inquiries a day on average, but this has now dropped to just one or two," the trader said. "On the West Coast, most inquiries have been seen

from Kandla and Mundra, mainly for bulk and container vessels. We have not seen any significant fresh demand on the East Coast recently."
"In September, inquiries were slightly lower than in August," the trader said. "This dip may be attributed to India's VLSFO pricing, which is $100 higher than in Singapore and Fujairah. As a result, we lost some volume in September. On the supply side, we had no issues throughout the month and saw good product availability. The average stem size was 400 metric tons, and the total volume at Kandla Port was around 35,000 mt."
Platts assessed marine fuel oil 0.5% delivered to Mumbai at $972/mt on Oct. 8, up $27/mt month over month. Marine fuel oil 0.5% delivered to Kandla was assessed at $977/mt, up $23/mt over the same period.
Kochi supply disruptions High sulfur fuel oil demand "was moderate for September,
even though there were supply constraints in Kochi," a Gujaratbased trader said.
A source close to Bharat Petroleum Corp. Ltd. (BPCL) said supply in Kochi was "somewhat inconsistent in September, with product on and off throughout the month ... Overall, BPCL's industrial volume in Kochi was around 5,700 mt in September."
Platts assessed marine fuel oil 0.5% delivered to Kochi at $1,001/mt on Oct. 8, up $31/mt month over month, while marine fuel oil 0.5% delivered to Colombo was assessed at $983/mt, up $33/mt over the same period.
East Coast bunker volumes mixed East Coast bunker activity showed divergent trends in
September, with stronger volumes in Chennai and higher HSFO demand in Paradip offset by operational constraints in Haldia and supply tightness at Tuticorin, according to market sources.
"We observed a decline in inquiries for September, primarily due to high prices in India," a Paradip-based supplier said. "Additionally, vessel movements were disrupted by weather issues in the last week of the month. During September, we supplied nearly 9,000 metric tons, with the average stem size ranging from 400 to 500 metric tons. Most of the demand was driven by vessels carrying coal, iron and fertilizers."

Bunker fuel demand at the Sri Lankan ports of Colombo, Trincomalee and Hambantota was strong in September, with consistent product availability and demand shifting from India to Lanka, according to regional traders and local suppliers.
"Supply and demand were stable in September, with good availability of product throughout the month. The average stem size was around 400-500 mt in Colombo, Trincomalee and Hambantota ports,'' a Sri Lanka-based supplier told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 8.
The primary demand in Sri Lanka is driven by container vessel traffic, with Colombo Port handling the largest volume. However, the Sri Lanka-based supplier said that some supplies

were disrupted at Colombo Port due to weather-related issues. Container traffic at Colombo was affected by these weather conditions at the outer port limit (OPL).
Supply and demand conditions have remained stable across all three grades at Sri Lankan ports. However, demand at Trincomalee port was high due to a shift from Indian ports in September.
"Overall, September was a good month, with strong product availability. However, we experienced weather-related issues in Colombo, which have affected the volume. Earlier, the volume at OPL was between 25,000 mt and 30,000 mt. Due to these weather conditions, OPL's volumes were restricted to 10,000 mt in September," another Colombo-based trader said.
"In total, the industrial volume in Sri Lanka was 97,000 mt, with Colombo contributing 75,000 mt, Hambantota was about 5,000 mt, and Trincomalee approximately 15,000 mt," the trader added.

Platts European Fuel Oil Daily Market Analysis
VLSFO arbitrage fixtures boost prices
HSFO demand remains weak amid volatility
Very low sulfur fuel oil markets strengthened on healthy arbitrage fixtures Oct. 8, while HSFO experienced limited demand.
The paper VLSFO market rebounded after a weaker start to October, with market participants seeing some arbitrage volumes leaving the region.
With a well-supplied European VLSFO market and wider EastWest paper spreads, the arbitrage has become more economic.

"There has been some volume fixed," said a European trader. However, dirty tanker freight rates remained extremely elevated, thereby challenging the viability of this arbitrage. Within the LSFO market, an additional market participant pointed to higher demand for 1% product into the AmsterdamRotterdam-Antwerp 0.5% fuel oil blending pool. The Northwest European high sulfur fuel oil market continued to be described by market participants as marked by limited demand Traders attributed the limited appetite for product to volatility in Brent futures and fluctuating ZRE-A ticket prices in Rotterdam. On the supply side no issues were reported, although some market participants raised concerns about the prompt availability of on-specification RMG product. This followed the arrival of volumes from Latin America in September. In the Mediterranean, there were opposing views regarding supply, with some traders pointing to tightness in the market, while others were observing a more balanced landscape. In the Platts Market on Close assessment process, Galtrade offered an HSFO cargo CIF basis Algeciras, but did not find a buyer. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
