Gas Liquids
NWE butane hits multiyear highs as winter gasoline demand squeezes merchant supply
Refiners retain butane, squeeze merchant supply
FOB seagoing prices reach highest since 2022
Low Rhine levels worsen inland logistics strain

Strength in European naphtha continued Oct. 8, with flat prices jumping sharply as gasoline blending demand pulled on the prompt market.
The strength was "still coming from gasoline," a trading source said, pointing to firm demand for higher-value blending components. North Sea light virgin naphtha was heard "going nuts" at elevated premiums, while heavy virgin naphtha for blending was reported higher too.
The move came despite indications of active prompt supply. The trading source said there were "lots of VPR traded," as well as "lots of North Sea" barrels and some major-origin barrels in the market. However, the available supply appeared to be absorbed by the blending pool, keeping prompt values supported.
The curve steepened across the structure, with the Mo1/Mo2 spread seeing the sharpest rise day over day, reflecting stronger nearby demand and continued prompt tightness despite active trading.
The front-month crack also edged higher again, extending its recent upward momentum as naphtha continued to strengthen relative to crude.
The renewed gasoline blending pull contrasts with softer petrochemical demand. Naphtha buying from steam crackers

The Northwest European butane complex reached multimonth and multiyear highs Oct. 8, as strong winter gasoline blending demand and refinery retention tightened merchant availability, driving FOB seagoing prices to their highest level in more than four years.
The strength spanned seaborne cargoes and AmsterdamRotterdam-Antwerp barges, with buyers facing limited spot supply as refiners prioritized internal consumption.
Seasonal blending requirements continued to absorb production, leaving fewer barrels available to meet demand outside the refinery system.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed FOB NWE seagoing butane at $1,144/metric ton, Oct. 8, its highest since March 9, 2022, when the assessment stood at $1,146.25/mt.
CIF NWE seagoing butane reached $1,061/mt, its highest since April 7, 2026, when it was assessed at $1,102/mt. CIF NWE large cargo butane stood at $931.75/mt, also its highest since April 7, when the assessment was $1,003.75/mt.
FOB ARA barges were assessed at $1,063/mt, their highest since the April 7, 2026, assessment of $1,133/mt.
The price milestones reflected a sustained squeeze across the regional market, with winter blending demand drawing on already restricted availability.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

Winter gasoline demand The transition to winter-grade gasoline remained the principal
demand driver. Higher permitted vapor pressure allowed greater incorporation of butane into the gasoline pool, supporting consumption from refiners and independent blenders.
Refinery retention intensified the pressure on external buyers. More production remained within refinery systems to satisfy captive blending requirements, reducing the volumes released into the merchant market and limiting opportunities to secure prompt parcels.
The seasonal increase in consumption therefore, coincided with a reduction in freely available supply. Buyers with outstanding requirements faced a narrow pool of material, while limited fresh spot offerings provided little relief.
Import availability also offered limited scope to ease the shortage. Few fresh US volumes were heard entering the regional spot market, leaving buyers reliant on constrained European availability. The absence of a more substantial replenishment flow reinforced the importance of refinery releases in meeting merchant requirements.
The resulting tightness supported prices across parcel sizes. Strength in the barge market accompanied elevated seagoing values, reflecting procurement difficulties across both inland and coastal supply chains.
Logistical pressure Low Rhine water levels added to the strain, restricting barge
loadings and reducing the amount of product that could move inland on each voyage.
Smaller parcels increased transport requirements, while constrained truck and rail capacity limited buyers' alternatives.
Those restrictions compounded the underlying shortage by making available material harder to deliver.
"The problem is still market tightness," a market source said. The source reported "not much buying or selling, because of this tightness" the source added. The comments reflected a market in which quieter trading did not necessarily signal weaker demand. Overall in the Northwest European market, sustained winter gasoline requirements, restricted refinery releases and difficult inland logistics kept the balance tight, underpinning the complex's return to levels last seen months -- and, for FOB seagoing butane, years -- earlier.
-- Baran Serdaroglu
Persian Gulf-Japan VLGC freight hits record high; LPG trade faces disruptions
Persian Gulf-Japan VLGC rate rises to $256.25/mt
Hormuz and Bab al-Mandab ship traffic drops
The Persian Gulf-Japan Very Large Gas Carrier freight rate rose more than 4% day over day to a record high of $256.25/metric ton on Oct. 7, with maritime disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz

and the Bab al-Mandab Strait, stemming from the Middle East conflict, limiting LPG cargo flows from the region.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Persian Gulf-Japan VLGC rate at $256.25/mt at the Asian close on Oct. 7, up 4.4%, or $10.90/mt, from Oct. 6. This was the highest rate for the route since the VLGC assessment was launched in December 2004.
According to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, LPG loadings in the Middle East rose to 1.936 million mt in September from 1.607 million mt in August. However, volumes remained well below the 4.284 million mt exported in September 2025.
Overall, ship traffic through the Strait of Hormuz fell to 19 transits on Oct. 6, marking a second straight decline from 23 transits, following attacks on vessels in the narrow waterway, the latest CAS report showed.
Five tankers were struck in the Strait of Hormuz over Oct. 3-6, with one vessel turning around after it was told to do so by the navy of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, according to multiple notices from the UK Maritime Trade Operations and Vanguard Tech.
Just beyond the Strait of Hormuz at the US blockade line, 58 vessels crossed the line, with 31 of them entering the Gulf and moving toward the Strait of Hormuz, CAS said.
Across the Arabian Peninsula, traffic at the region's other maritime chokepoint totaled 40 vessels, with 16 moving south through the Bab al-Mandab Strait, according to CAS.
Ship-to-ship transfer activity in the Middle East has risen as geopolitical tensions and logistical constraints at the Strait of Hormuz have supported greater use of such transfers, according to shipbrokers.
QatarEnergy sold a 26,000-26,500 mt fully refrigerated propane cargo via tender for Sept. 24-28 delivery through ship-toship transfer at Vadinar, India, at a premium in the $120s/mt over Saudi Aramco contract prices, Platts reported Sept. 24, citing trade sources.

Platts European Propane Daily Commentary
Transport woes tighten NW Europe supply
Propane prices jump up to $55/mt daily
Petrochemical demand stays firm despite costs
Distribution difficulties sharpened the focus on accessible propane supply in Northwest Europe Oct. 8, as restricted inland transport and limited import options complicated supply while petrochemical demand remained supported.
"Rhine is impacting," a market source said, highlighting the logistical pressures facing inland customers. Lower bargecarrying capacity increased the number of movements required to fulfill deliveries, placing additional strain on scheduling. Truck and rail alternatives offered limited relief as demand for transport capacity remained high.
Seaborne replenishment also faced obstacles. The USto-Europe arbitrage remained closed, while Asian demand competed for Atlantic Basin exports.
Northwest European consumers consequently depended heavily on North Sea suppliers and regional refineries, leaving fewer alternatives.
Refinery fuel requirements further restricted the pool of propane reaching external buyers. Elevated natural gas costs encouraged internal LPG use, reducing refinery contributions to merchant availability.
Petrochemical demand remained supported despite propane's narrowing discount to naphtha. The propane-naphtha spread remained more negative than the minus-$60/metric ton switching level indicated by market participants, beyond which naphtha would become increasingly attractive.

Platts European Butane Daily Commentary
Gasoline blending tightens butane supply
CIF large cargo prices jump $40/mt to $932
Low Rhine levels complicate barge logistics
Winter gasoline production kept pressure on Northwest European butane availability on Oct. 8, as refiners' internal requirements absorbed material that would otherwise have supplied the spot market.
The seasonal relaxation of gasoline vapor-pressure limits increased the scope for butane inclusion in blending pools. This strengthened competition for available tons while encouraging producers to retain output, reducing the surplus offered to external customers.
As a result, merchant buyers faced a restricted choice of parcels. Limited selling interest constrained opportunities to conclude spot supply.
The balance between gasoline and petrochemical consumption also remained important. Butane's stronger performance against naphtha had eroded its appeal as a cracking feedstock, leaving gasoline blending as the more supportive outlet.
Reduced discretionary petrochemical buying offered some counterweight to tightness, but did not directly resolve shortages of suitably located merchant material.

Logistics added another obstacle to procurement. Low Rhine water levels restricted barge carrying capacity, complicating inland replenishment and increasing reliance on alternative transport.
The resulting pressure on truck and rail availability made delivery arrangements an important consideration alongside product availability.
The prospect of US replenishment remained dependent on workable delivered economics, freight and arrival timing.
Overseas supply therefore offered no automatic solution to immediate requirements.
Attention centred on whether additional refinery sales or import arrivals could restore flexibility to the spot market as winter-grade gasoline production continued.
In the butane CIF NWE large cargo market, no bids, offers or trades were heard in the Platts Market on Close assessment process. In the absence of competitive indications, the market was assessed on trader sentiment.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the CIF large cargo market in outright terms at $931.75/mt, up $40/mt day over day and stable as a percentage to naphtha at 101%.
In the absence of indications in the MOC, the butane coaster market was assessed on trader sentiment.
Platts assessed the FOB Seagoing butane coaster market at $1,144/mt, stable day over day versus naphtha at 124%.
Platts assessed the CIF Seagoing market at $1,061/mt, up $72/mt day over day and up 3 percentage versus naphtha at 115%. This put the FOB coasters at a $83/mt premium to the CIF coaster market.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Baran Serdaroglu
Platts Western Mediterranean LPG Daily Commentary
Refineries retain butane for blending needs
West Med butane jumps $147/mt to $1,158
Replacement costs and limited refinery sales shaped the West Mediterranean butane market on Oct. 8, with buyers facing a narrow range of supply options as seasonal demand competed for available production.
Sonatrach Spa's increase in October contract prices raised the underlying cost of Algerian supply for contracts linked to its monthly benchmark. The adjustment provided a firmer replacement-cost reference across the region.
Alongside that higher cost base, refinery retention restricted the volume available for coastal distribution.
Refineries prioritized their own gasoline blending requirements as winter specifications allowed greater butane incorporation, leaving less material available for discretionary sales into the merchant market.
Therefore, limited spot supply offered little indication of easing fundamentals.

Tankers
Hormuz shipping traffic rises Oct. 7 but remains below September weekly averages
Hormuz traffic rises to 23 ships Oct. 7
Tanker suffers attack near Qatar coastline
7-day averages below previous transit volumes
Traffic through the Strait of Hormuz edged up Oct. 7 to 23 transits from 19 the previous day, but 7-day transit averages remain below levels seen throughout most of September, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities At Sea.
In the first week of October, the average transit count was 22 per day, compared with 27 ships transiting the contested waterway per day on average in the final week of September. However, transit levels are slightly higher than the first week of September when transits averaged around 19 vessels each day.
A resurgence of ship attacks has kept traffic through the

narrow waterway low, with another attack off Qatar's coast reported overnight Oct. 7. The Antigua and Barbuda-flagged oil and chemical tanker Acers was struck by multiple projectiles, Vanguard Tech reported Oct. 8. It is the first attack in weeks to occur deep inside Persian Gulf waters, with most recent attacks concentrated around the Strait of Hormuz, UKMTO data showed.
Three crude and three product tankers were among the vessels that went through the strait Oct. 7, the CAS report said. Most of the vessels that transited the Strait of Hormuz were Iranlinked, while nine ships were unsanctioned, the report said.
Just beyond the Strait of Hormuz at the US blockade line, 65 vessel transits were tracked, on par with the seven-day average of 63, with a roughly even split of vessels moving each way across the line, the report said. Four bulk carriers that recently called at Iranian ports crossed the blockade line, with three moving outbound, the report said.
Traffic at the Bab al-Mandab Strait comprised 16 tankers, nine bulk carriers, 10 container ships and eight miscellaneous vessels, according to the report.
-- Lauren Holtmeier, Claudia Carpenter
LR2 tanker demand rises 28% as crude cargoes offset Hormuz clean-product loss
Clean-product volumes on LR2s fall 28% in first nine months Ton-mile demand increases 2% despite the disruption Fleet capacity rises18% as crude-cargo dependence deepens
Long Range 2 tanker demand in 2026 has grown 28% year over year, pivoting toward crude oil and heavy products after disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz eliminated more than a third of the sector's traditional clean-product business, shipping association BIMCO said Oct. 8.
More than 40% of clean-product volumes carried by LR2s in 2025 were loaded in the Persian Gulf, equivalent to 1.2 million barrels/day. Following the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz, that figure fell to 400,000 b/d in 2026 so far, or 18% of total clean-product volumes loaded on LR2s, BIMCO said.
S&P Global Commodities at Sea data showed Persian Gulf exports of clean refined products fell to 2 million b/d in 2026 to date, from 3.7 million b/d in 2025.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

30

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

"Despite the lower volumes, ton-mile demand increased 2% year on year," Niels Rasmussen, chief shipping analyst at BIMCO, said.
LR2s loaded an average of 2.1 million b/d of clean products during the first nine months of 2026, down 800,000 b/d year over year, BIMCO said. The shortfall was more than offset by crude oil and heavy-product liftings, which averaged 5.1 million b/d, up 1.8 million b/d, BIMCO data showed.
Overall, LR2s loaded 1 million b/d more cargo during the period than in the same period of 2025, an increase of 15%, Rasmussen said.
Limited ton-mile gains Clean tanker companies have emphasized the boost to clean
tanker business that longer voyages bring, as the Middle East conflict reshuffles trade routes. Extra ton-miles on the back of vessels serving new destinations by more elongated routes have gone some way to blunting the impact of lower clean product cargoes, Carlos Balestra di Mottola, CEO of clean tanker company d'Amico, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, in September.
However, the average sailing distance for LR2 cargoes in 2026 has fallen 12% year over year, BIMCO said. Longer-haul Persian Gulf clean-product trades were replaced by shorter crude oil and heavy-product routes, BIMCO said.
The average sailing distance for crude oil and heavy products carried by LR2s was 33% shorter than for clean products, BIMCO said.
The Americas and Mediterranean were the primary drivers of the cargo shift, together accounting for 80% of the increase in crude oil and heavy-product volumes loaded on LR2s, BIMCO said.
The Platts global LR2 index, for non-eco, non-scrubber-fitted vessels, was $241,013/day Oct. 7, compared with $305,008/day for the equivalent Aframax index. Since the indices were launched in November, they have averaged $83,397/day and $84,509/day, respectively.
Fleet growth The cargo pivot has coincided with a significant expansion
of the LR2 fleet. Capacity has grown 18% since Q2 2025, driven by a large order book and limited recycling activity, BIMCO said.

BIMCO expects fleet growth to continue. The increase in crude oil and heavy product volumes carried
by LR2s since then has coincided with that expansion in LR2 fleet capacity, Rasmussen said. "We therefore expect LR2s' dependence on crude oil and heavy-product exports to continue," he said.
Clean tanker freight should remain elevated over the next two weeks, although momentum may moderate as prompt tonnage increases, S&P Global Energy Horizons analysts said Oct. 7. East of Suez, LR1 and LR2 rates should remain supported by Strait of Hormuz risks, Persian Gulf premiums and firm eastbound and westbound demand, the analysts said.
-- Thomas Washington, Alara Tizer
Platts East of Suez Clean Tanker Daily Commentary
Hormuz concerns push up freight again
MR North Asia freight unchanged
East of Suez long-range tankers were higher for the second successive trading day, Oct. 8, due to a higher risk perception inside the Strait of Hormuz and also an anticipation that some more LR2s may turn dirty in the next few weeks to garner up their earnings, market participants said.
There was market chatter that an LR1 fixture may have been done around w925, with a tanker being placed on subjects on one of the benchmark Persian Gulf-North Asia routes for loading inside the Strait of Hormuz, but neither details nor confirmation were available. "The chatter was heard, but it could not be confirmed," said a chartering source with a global commodities trading company.
However, Market participants noted that the rate signaled a bullish trend ahead, with expectations of a forthcoming rally. A broker in Singapore said the upward potential was due to risky loadings inside the Persian Gulf, which only a section of the fleet is prepared to undertake, and the switchover of several tankers to dirty.
Even for a much shorter voyage on the Yanbu-UKC route with relatively lesser risks, Unipec placed an LR2, the Elandra Sound, on subjects at a whopping $12 million for Oct. 22 diesel loading with an all-time high demurrage at $300,000, sources said.
Even when the US-Iran War was on an upswing in early June, an LR2 fixture for loading inside the Persian Gulf had been done for around $10 million, and the comparison shows that freight is much higher now.
Loadings inside the Persian Gulf still command a significant premium over the Gulf of Oman. LR1s are expected to be expensive inside the Persian Gulf as the market participants are cautious due to several recent attacks while transiting the Strait of Hormuz. A section of the shipping industry believes that this should make transportation across the risky waters even more costly. Owners willing to move their ships through the Strait of Hormuz are seeking higher freight rates due to heightened risk perception, while charterers insist that lower demand should

Aframax fundamentals strengthen
The East of Suez VLCC market remained firm Oct. 8, with fundamentals continuing to favor owners amid a tightening position list and numerous vessels reported to be on subjects, sources said.

Meanwhile, a market source said strength in the Atlantic continued to provide further support, with VLCC rates for voyages loading on the US Gulf Coast and in Brazil exceeding last-done levels, while daily earnings caught up with those in the Oman market.
The Suezmax market remained firm, but assessed rates were unchanged day over day. A Suezmax broker said charterers could be holding back after the sharp increase in rates. No fresh cargoes were heard, sources said.
The Aframax sector saw sharply higher rates Oct. 8, as numerous participants pointed to strong fundamentals across both the Persian Gulf and Southeast Asia. A broker said cargoes loading in the Persian Gulf were still being worked privately, and rates could therefore climb further.

added that charterers were seeking to downsize into medium range tankers, with Aframax availability remaining tight.
Among fresh cargoes, Shell was heard seeking an Aframax for an Oct. 27 loading from Singapore to Noumea, with an option to discharge in Papua New Guinea. BCPT was heard seeking an Aframax for an Oct. 27 loading from Linggi to Thailand. Recent cargoes heard loading in Australia had yet to be covered, multiple brokers said.
Medium range rates were mostly firm day over day. Recent cargoes were still being worked, sources said. In fixture activity, Safesea Varuna was heard taken by United at an undisclosed rate for an Oct. 20 loading from Singapore to Chittagong.

Dangote refinery takes IPO to East Africa with Kenya, Uganda approvals
Dangote cleared to sell shares in Uganda and Kenya
IPO targets $1.6 billion to help fund refinery expansion
Refinery supplied 20% of Europe's jet fuel in summer
Nigeria's Dangote refinery business has sought new East African investment through its landmark IPO, following approval to sell shares in some of the region's largest markets.
After launching its closely watched IPO on the Nigerian Stock Exchange on Sept. 14, the Lagos-based refining business on Oct. 6 was granted approval to sell shares in both Kenya and Uganda, according to announcements from the countries' respective market authorities.
A statement from the Ugandan market authority confirmed that Dangote had been authorized to promote, market and distribute shares to eligible investors via its licensed intermediaries, after verifying that the group's IPO prospectus had been approved by the Nigerian Securities and Exchange Commission.
The Kenyan authority also issued a statement listing approved entities to coordinate participation, including the National Bank of Kenya and Nairobi-based investment bank Sterling Capital Limited.
The approvals will allow regional investors to participate in what the business has branded "the people's IPO," with its sights set on Nigerians around the world, as well as a range of other African investors. With an offering of 4.1 billion ordinary shares, the company hopes to raise more than $1.6 billion, representing what would be the largest IPO in Africa to date.
Funding will be used to advance a $12 billion expansion project at the company's 700,000 barrels/day refinery in Lekki, near Lagos, which was first commissioned in 2024 and is set to double in scale by 2029. Post-expansion, the facility will be capable of processing 1.4 million b/d of crude, more than any other site globally, and will have the capacity to produce 2.4 million metric tons/year of polypropylene.
The company is also advancing a second $16 billion refinery project in Lamu, Kenya, where it intends to construct a 700,000 b/d plant by January 2030, alongside a new fuel tank farm in Namibia. It has also shared plans to build pipelines linking

Djibouti and Ethiopia, and running from Namibia to Zambia and Zimbabwe.
Speaking at the UAE's Fujairah Energy Markets Forum on Oct. 6, Dangote refinery CEO David Bird said the company was aiming for 10 million retail subscribers through the IPO, more than double the 4.5 million that took part in Aramco's 2019 listing, and said it was "well on the way" to its target.
"A lot of effort has been put into the ease of uptake, the ease of participation," Bird said, calling the IPO primarily a means to encourage participation in the business. "We're not seeking to raise money. Arguably, we're giving up value."
The company has seen a surge in profitability since ramping up its Nigerian refinery to full capacity for the first time this year, and emerging as a major global exporter at a time when the Middle East war has sent fuel prices soaring. While restricted shipping traffic in the Strait of Hormuz squeezed conventional sources of middle distillate products, Dangote met 20% of Europe's jet fuel demand in June, July and August, Bird told the conference.
In its IPO prospectus, Dangote said the refining business had swung into profit in the first half of 2026, taking home $1.82 billion after tax, contrasting with two years of losses in 2024 and 2025.
-- Kelly Norways, Namala Doreen
Most USGC refineries set to avoid direct hit from hurricane; exports affected
Storm narrows refinery threat to 500,000 b/d
Ports restrict ship movement ahead of landfall
Diesel exports drop 700,000 b/d week over week
The US Gulf Coast's refinery row is likely to be spared a direct hit from Hurricane Isaias, but residual impacts from port closures and regional shipping delays are likely.
Direct impacts to US Gulf Coast refineries have been whittled down to just under 500,000 barrels/day of capacity in Alabama and Mississippi Oct. 8, down from 2.5 million b/d expected earlier this week, when the storm was seen possibly affecting refineries as far west as New Orleans and Baton Rouge.
Hurricane Isaias is forecast to strengthen to a Category 2 hurricane with winds of up to 110 miles per hour before weakening slightly ahead of landfall along the northern Gulf Coast late Friday, according to the National Hurricane Center latest Forecast Discussion Oct. 8 at 4:00 pm CT.
Southern Mississippi is expected to face hurricane-force winds, prolonged power outages and heavy rainfall, while eastern parts of the state could see damaging inland wind gusts and flash flooding.
The NHC also warned of a life-threatening storm surge threat extending west to the Mouth of the Mississippi River and significant river flooding across the Gulf Coast region through the weekend.
The US Coast Guard Captain of the Port on Oct. 8 changed the status of ports within the Mobile, Alabama, purview to Port

Condition Yankee, which prompts ports to restrict or suspend ship movements, require ships not involved in cargo operations to depart, and accelerate preparations for potential port closure and severe weather impacts on maritime traffic.
"Tropical Storm Isaias is forecast to bring gale-force winds (39-73 mph) into Sector Mobile's area of responsibility within 24 hours," the Coast Guard's Marine Safety Information Bulletin, released at 8 am CT, said.
Mobile port operations include the port of Pascagoula, home to Chevron's 356,440 b/d refinery, the largest refinery within the hurricane warning zone.

Dispatches have been blocked from the Donges refinery in the northwest, Gonfreville in Normandy and the biorefinery La Mede in the south.

The union called in mid-September for a strike on Oct. 8 at refineries and some retail stations, saying it could result in blocking the dispatch of oil products from refineries or the closure of retail stations.
The strike is over working conditions, which have worsened during the heat wave, as well as over pay. The strike is scheduled to last 24 hours.
TotalEnergies said in a statement Oct. 8 that it continues to ensure supplies to its network of retail stations and its customers through its various logistics channels.
It said its four refineries, including one biorefinery, and seven storage facilities are operating normally.
The company told local media that only 13 out of 3,300 retail stations have been affected.

Smaller clean fuel quota allocation Market sources pegged China's latest batch of clean oil
product export quotas at about 4.5 million mt, released before the holiday, significantly below pre-holiday expectations of around 8 million mt.
"The new quotas mean only around 2 million mt of oil products can still be exported for a month, which would be a sharp decline from August and September levels," a Beijing-based analyst said.
The allocation would also be substantially lower than the 8.395 million mt issued in the third batch of clean oil product export quotas in mid-September 2025.
Platts reported Sept. 24 that this year's third-round allocation was likely to be smaller than the corresponding batch issued a year earlier, implying a volume of less than 8 million mt.
China's clean oil product exports surged 79.6% month over month to 4.58 million mt, or 1.16 million barrels/day, in August, the highest level since January 2023, as shipments of jet fuel, gasoil and gasoline all increased, according to customs data.
Third LSFO quota batch issued China has allocated a third batch of low sulfur fuel oil export
quotas totaling 1.07 million mt to state-owned oil companies, sources with knowledge of the matter said Oct. 8.
The latest issuance lifted China's year-to-date LSFO export quotas to 14.07 million mt, up 5.6% from a year earlier.
-- Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialist
Malaysia calls for stronger regional cooperation on oil supply security
AZEC nations discuss Hormuz vulnerabilities
Members explore voluntary oil storage boosts
Malaysia called for stronger regional cooperation on oil supply security and low-carbon energy transition at the 4th Ministerial Meeting of the Asia Zero Emissions Community in Manila Oct. 8, as geopolitical risks to key crude shipping routes, including the Strait of Hormuz, dominated discussions among member states.
Economy Minister Akmal Nasrullah bin Haji Mohd Nasir, who represented Malaysia at the AZEC gathering, said the energy crisis was exposing the limits of unilateral action.
"The energy crisis is testing countries' ability to cooperate without leaving anyone behind in the transition to a low-carbon economy," he said in a statement.
The meeting drew all 11 AZEC member states -- Japan, Australia, Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand and Vietnam -- and was cochaired by Japan's State Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry Kei Takagi and Philippines Energy Secretary Sharon Garin.
Hormuz concerns The Strait of Hormuz featured prominently on the agenda,
with members discussing the vulnerability of supply routes amid ongoing geopolitical uncertainty.

The strait is a critical chokepoint for Middle East crude exports to Asia, with the bulk of Gulf producer shipments to Japan, South Korea, China and India transiting the waterway.
Members discussed voluntary increases in oil storage capacity, subject to national circumstances and capabilities, as a buffer against potential supply disruptions, the statement said.
The meeting also addressed cooperation under the POWERR Asia initiative and concluded with a meeting involving Saudi Arabia and the UAE to explore opportunities in petroleum supply security strategies.
Malaysia's bilateral engagements on the sidelines underscored the country's dual focus on near-term supply security and longer-term decarbonization.
Akmal Nasrullah held talks with Australia's Assistant Minister for Climate Change and Energy Josh Wilson, centering on energy security and gas supply -- a pairing that reflects Malaysia's interest in deepening LNG trade ties with Canberra.
A separate bilateral with Takagi covered Malaysia's role within AZEC and cooperation prospects in carbon capture, utilization and storage and hydrogen.
On the sidelines, Akmal Nasrullah witnessed the signing of a memorandum of understanding between Japan's IHI Corp. and TNB Power Generation Sdn. Bhd., the generation arm of Malaysian utility Tenaga Nasional Bhd., for a feasibility study on ammonia fuel development.
A letter of intent was also exchanged between METI and Malaysia's Ministry of Energy Transition and Water Transformation covering the energy transition.
-- Angelica Garcia
AZEC ministers commit to energy security, adopt work program for oil stockpiling
Work program to support oil stockpile capacity building
Invite Saudi Arabia, UAE to speak on oil stockpiling
Gas, LNG facilitate energy transition while ensuring energy security
Ministers from the Asia Zero Emission Community underscored their commitment to bolstering energy security and supply chain resilience, and to laying out a work program to facilitate the building of oil stockpiles amid disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz, according to a joint statement adopted Oct. 8.
"We reaffirm our commitment to enhancing energy security and strengthening supply chain resilience in order to secure the stability of the region and contribute to global energy resilience," the ministers said following the end of the 4th AZEC Ministerial Meeting at the Philippine International Convention Center in Pasay City.
"We will explore ways to support resilient energy supply systems that can withstand energy supply disruptions, including by enhancing oil stockpiling capacity on a voluntary and commercial basis consistent with the principle under the ASEAN Framework Agreement on Petroleum Security (APSA),

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

36

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 8, 2026

where applicable, through coordination between producer and consumer countries."
Speaking at a joint press conference following the end of the AZEC Ministerial Meeting, Philippine Energy Secretary Sharon Garin said, "Recent disruptions in global energy markets, particularly at the critical choke point of the Strait of Hormuz, have reinforced the importance of strengthening our collective preparedness against energy supply shocks."
"One of the key outcomes of today's meeting is the adoption of the AZEC Ministerial work program for strengthening oil stockpiling," Garin said. "This will support closer cooperation and capacity building in oil reserve management, helping participating countries strengthen their ability to respond to potential supply disruptions."
Work program The ministers of the 11-nation AZEC partner countries of
Australia, Brunei Darussalam, Cambodia, Indonesia, Japan, Lao PDR, Malaysia, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand and Vietnam also welcomed the adoption of AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling.
"Recognizing that each country will pursue various pathways, taking into account its national circumstances, we will advance efforts to strengthen domestic oil stockpiling capacity and enhance regional cooperation, particularly through the following steps, while utilizing Partnership on Wide Energy and Resources Resilience (POWERR) Asia," according to the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document attached to the joint statement.
POWERR Asia, which was launched in April by Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi, is a cooperation framework comprising both emergency and structural responses with mid- to long-term perspectives.
"We also note that nothing in this Work Program would create obligations independent of, or in advance of, the ASEAN Framework Agreement on Petroleum Security (APSA) for AZEC partner countries," the statement said.
The ministers acknowledged the importance of APSA in guiding regional stockpiling initiatives and encouraged AZEC partner countries to make progress on strengthening their energy supply systems by coordinating policy recommendations or conducting projects related to the Work Program, taking into account different national circumstances, according to the joint statement.
First step The adoption of the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for
Strengthening Oil Stockpiling was a step forward in developing petroleum stockpiles among ASEAN countries after years of little progress made in developing oil stockpile frameworks in the region.
The AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document noted that the first step toward

strengthening domestic oil stockpiling capacity is the establishment of appropriate stockpiling and release systems.
To ensure sufficient stockpiling capacity, the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document noted that it is important to develop supportive infrastructure and systems such as storage tanks, import terminals, port facilities, pipelines, and other transportation infrastructure.
To expand oil stockpiling capacity, the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document noted that it is essential to cooperate with oil-producing countries, such as countries in the Middle East, on a voluntary basis while respecting national sovereignty to build more secure supply system.
Kei Takagi, Japan's State Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry, who co-chaired the meeting, said at the joint press conference, "Saudi Arabia and the UAE were invited to participate in the first-ever ministerial-level session between Middle Eastern oil-producing countries and AZEC partner countries."
"During the session, Middle Eastern countries expressed their willingness to cooperate in strengthening oil stockpiling capacity across Asia. The participants also confirmed that they would further deepen cooperation going forward to enhance energy security," Takagi added.
Energy diversification In the joint statement, AZEC ministers also recognized the
importance of pursuing diverse pathways toward decarbonization or net zero emissions while ensuring energy security, in accordance with national circumstances and policies.
"We emphasize that, in many partner countries where energy demand is increasing, gas and LNG can serve as important energy sources in facilitating the energy transition while ensuring energy security," according to the joint statement. "We recognize the role of gas and LNG in facilitating renewable energy integration and maintaining grid reliability."
The ministers also recognized the vital role of renewable energy in advancing the clean energy transition while enhancing energy security by reducing dependence on energy imports.
"The development of renewable energy systems, supported by battery energy storage systems (BESS), alongside smart grids, will be key to ensuring access to energy for all," according to the joint statement.
"The use of renewable energy is being encouraged to reduce reliance on electricity generated from fossil fuels. Innovative financing to make renewable energy, such as solar panels (PV) and BESS, affordable for households is also being promoted.
The ministers also recognized the use of sustainable biofuels in the transport sector can play an important role, as part of a diverse range of decarbonization pathways, in meeting growing fuel demand while reducing dependence on fossil fuels.
-- Takeo Kumagai, Angelica Garcia

OIL FUTURES: Tanker attacks, GOM shut-ins support oil even as Trump cools Iran escalation fears ...from page 1
constant risk of being attacked call into question the longerterm feasibility of the flows. This ultimately warrants a sticky risk premium to remain in pricing," TD Securities analysts Ryna McKay and Bart Melek said in an Oct. 8 note.
A resurgence of ship attacks has kept traffic through the Strait of Hormuz low, with another attack off Qatar's coast reported overnight Oct. 7. The Antigua and Barbuda-flagged oil and chemical tanker Acers was struck by multiple projectiles, Vanguard Tech reported Oct. 8. It is the first attack in weeks to occur deep inside Persian Gulf waters, with most recent attacks concentrated around the Strait of Hormuz, UKMTO data showed.
Traffic through the Strait of Hormuz edged up Oct. 7 to 23 transits from 19 the previous day, but 7-day transit averages remain below levels seen throughout most of September, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Tropical Storm Isaias shuts US Gulf of Mexico output Gulf of Mexico oil and gas production plummeted Oct. 8
as operators evacuated platforms ahead of Tropical Storm Isaias, which the National Weather Service forecasts will attain hurricane status as it approaches the northern Gulf Coast Oct. 9.
Operators had evacuated 121 platforms or 32.61% of total Gulf platforms ahead of the storm, the US Bureau of Safety and Environmental Enforcement reported Oct. 8. Total current shutins amount to approximately 1.28 million barrels/day of oil and 1.13 million cubic feet/day of natural gas production, representing 62.89% and 57.35% of total Gulf output respectively.
The US Gulf Coast's refinery row is likely to be spared a direct hit from Isaias, though around 500,000 b/d of capacity in Alabama and Mississippi could be impacted. However, residual impacts from port closures and regional shipping delays are likely, Platts, a part of S&P Global Energy, reported.
-- Christopher Vanmoessner
Refinery updates
REFINERY NEWS: Argentina's crude runs climb 9.1% on year in August
Diesel output shoots up 12%
Gasoline production rises 5%
Fuel oil output falls 2.8%
Argentinian crude oil runs shot up 9.1% in August year on year, led by a surge in diesel production, state statistics department Indec said late Oct. 7.
Production of diesel, the most consumed refined product in the country, shot up 12% in August on the year, Indec said in a data report that didn't provide raw data.
Diesel demand tends to rise for agriculture in August, with

farmers completing the planting of the 2026-27 wheat crop and sowing fields with 2026-27 corn and sunflower, while also harvesting the 2025-26 corn and sorghum crops, according to data from the Agriculture Secretariat.
Gasoline production, the second-most consumed refined product, increased 5%, Indec added.
Demand for petroleum products has been recovering on the whole as the economy grows following a 2023-24 recession, with growth of 4.4% in 2025 and an expected 1.5% this year, according to a survey of economists by the central bank.
State-run YPF has a 55% share of diesel and gasoline sales, trailed by Raizen, BP-backed Pan American Energy and Trafigura.
Argentina has about 560,000 b/d of installed refining capacity, but demand generally runs at 550,000 b/d. All of the crude is supplied domestically from fields that produced a record high of 932,000 b/d in August. Refiners import additional supplies of higher-grade products because of a lack of capacity to make them.
-- Charles Newbery

Owner: Rosneft

Several Russian refineries are back online or restarting operations, fully or partially, and have resumed sales of products on the St. Petersburg exchange, according to Russia-based market sources with knowledge of the matter.
The Moscow, Ryazan and Saratov refinery resumed sales of products on the exchange floor in late September, according to Russia-based market sources.
The Moscow refinery halted operations and sales on the exchange floor after a Sept. 20 attack, sources said previously. The Ukrainian military said it had hit the AVT-6 (CDU VDU 6) primary processing unit, the complex oil processing unit and the isomerization unit. The refinery is currently preparing for restart and has resumed sales of products, according to Russia-based market sources with knowledge of the matter.
Saratov is operating partially and has resumed sales of products, according to Russia-based market sources with knowledge of the matter. It has been offline and absent as a seller on the exchange floor, sources said previously. The

REFINERY NEWS: Iran's Isfahan undergoes partial maintenance
Refinery: Isfahan, Iran
Owner: National Iranian Oil Refining & Distribution Co., private investors
Overall capacity: 430,000 barrels/day
The catalytic reforming 1, visbreaker 1 and nitrogen production units at Iran's Isfahan oil refinery have been undergoing a basic overhaul since Oct. 3, according to the National Iranian Oil Products Refining and Distribution Co.'s website Oct. 8.
National Iranian Oil Products Refining and Distribution Co. is a subsidiary of National Iranian Oil Refining & Distribution Co., which owns the refinery.
"The repair of the turbine and gas compressor of the catalytic reforming unit C-251 is part of the special jobs in this round," Alireza Ghazvinizadeh, director for Repair and Maintenance, was quoted as saying by the public relations office of the plant.
The process will be completed within 20 days, it said. The diesel purification unit 1 in the southern section of the plant successfully completed an overhaul as of Oct. 3, the website showed. The other units at the refinery are in service without interruption, the website said.

REFINERY NEWS: Romania's Petromidia to develop photovoltaic power plant
Refinery: Petromidia, Navodari, Romania
Owner: Rompetrol Rafinare, owned by KMG International and the Romanian state
Overall capacity: 6 million mt/year (120,000 b/d)
Refinery: Vega in Ploiesti, Romania
Owner: Rompetrol Rafinare
Overall capacity: 400,000 mt/year
Romania's Rompetrol said Oct. 8 that it is launching a project to develop a photovoltaic plant with an installed capacity of 9.4 megawatts at its Petromidia refinery.
"The photovoltaic plant is expected to generate more than 9,800 megawatt-hours of renewable electricity annually, all of which will be used by the refinery," it said.
The project is scheduled for implementation by Dec. 31, 2028. The company also said that in recent years, Petromidia has "implemented significant projects to reduce environmental impact, increase energy efficiency, optimize technological processes, and strengthen the platform's energy independence," with a flagship project being the new cogeneration plant, which entered commercial operation at the end of 2025. As a result of continuous investments and upgrades, Petromidia achieved utilization of 98.11% in the first half of 2026 and a light products yield of 86.54%. Rompetrol has also launched a project to develop a photovoltaic plant with an installed capacity of 4.8 MW and an energy storage system of about 9 MWh at the Vega refinery. Rompetrol's Vega specialty plant is a bitumen, hexane and solvents producer that processes distilled products from the Petromidia refinery.
-- Elza Turner

The ministry posted a video Oct. 8 on X from Ukrainian media outlet exilenova_plus, described as "fresh footage of a strike by Ukraine's Defense Forces on Gazprom's Salavat oil refinery."
"A fire broke out on the territory of the enterprise," the Ukrainian military said in a Telegram post, adding that Gazprom's Salavat is one of the largest oil-refining and petrochemical complexes in Russia.
The mayor of Salavat, Marat Zagidullin, confirmed the attack on the city but did not mention the refinery. "The debris of several drones fell in the industrial area and a fire broke out," Zagidullin said in a post on Telegram.
The Salavat refinery produces gasoline, diesel, fuel oil, bitumen, polyethylene and other oil and petrochemical products, and supplies Russia's armed forces, the Ukrainian military said.
In a separate post Oct. 8, the Ukrainian military confirmed the attack on the Omsk refinery, the largest refining facility in Russia 2,500 km away from Ukraine. Russian media outlet Astra reported on the Omsk refinery attack, citing information provided by witnesses.
"One drone reached the northern industrial hub. Emergency services are working at the scene," Omsk region Governor Vitaliy Khotsenko wrote in a Telegram post, without providing any further details about the attack.
"The Omsk Oil Refinery had previously been struck on July 6, 2026. At that time, Ukrainian forces hit the ELOU-AVT-11 primary oil processing unit, which has a design capacity of 8.4 million tons of oil per year," the Ukrainian military said.
Salavat was last attacked by Ukraine on Aug. 13. The refinery was partially operational at the end of September, according to sources.
Salavat continued selling products on the exchange floor Oct. 8, although in smaller volumes, according to Russia-based market sources. Omsk was selling products on the exchange Oct. 8, according to the sources.
Of Russia's primary refining capacity, 3.1 million barrels/day, or about 45% of the total, was offline as of the end of September, according to S&P Global Energy CERA estimates.

Refinery: La Rabida, Huelva, southwest Spain
Owner: Moeve

Moeve, which is the largest shareholder in the Onuba project and operator of the Huelva Energy Park where it is located.

The total volume is 500 GWh per year, the reports said, citing officials from both companies. It did not publish the price or the term details.
The supply will come from two solar PV plants in the region: 265 GWh from La Luz, which is in operation, and 235 GWh from Puerto Sostenible, subject to the completion of the plant.
The Onubo project is already under construction and will have an initial 300 MW of electrolysis capacity, with expansion capacity for a further 105 MW. Its output is expected to be 45,000 metric tons/yr of renewable hydrogen, which would offset 250,000 mt/yr of carbon dioxide emissions, according to the report.
Moeve announced the start of construction of Onuba, which should start producing in 2029 to feed renewable fuels for road, maritime, and aviation customers, while providing feedstock for the Chemicals business, it said in September.
Thyssenkrupp Nucera was contracted by Moeve for engineering, procurement, fabrication and supply of 15 standardized 20 MW alkaline water electrolysis electrolyzer units.
The plant has been recognized as a Project of Common European Interest (PCI) by the European Commission and was approved for around 1 billion in public funding.
Spain's government on Sept. 30 announced it would support Onuba and a 100 MW hydrogen project at the Bilbao refinery by fast-tracking public consultations for the hydrogen transport grid connecting the two refineries to local offtakers.

Platts proposes amending freight calculation for Oman, Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the freight calculation used in its daily Oman and Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
Under the proposed change, Platts would use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (DOOEW00), which was launched Aug. 3, in its calculation of the outright price of Oman and Murban delivered into North Asia at 4:30 pm Singapore time (0830 GMT).
The proposed change would better reflect the shipping economics for the two grades, with Murban primarily loading from Fujairah port and Oman from Mina Al Fahal port outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Currently, the freight calculation is based on the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (TDAAB00).
The freight calculation would continue to use the conversion factor for metric tons to barrels, as applied to the grades, in line with their prevailing qualities. The conversion factors are currently 7.32 for Oman and 7.62 for Murban.
In line with this change, Platts is also proposing to use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East worldscale freight assessment (DIOEW00) in the freight calculation for Murban in its Indonesian Minas, Cinta and Widuri crude market parity prices.
Under the current MPP methodology for Minas, Cinta and Widuri, Murban -- as one of five basket grades -- is normalized for delivery to Singapore, accounting for freight. This is currently calculated using the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Singapore worldscale freight assessment (PFAOD00).
The affected assessments appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Oilgram Price Report, LNG Daily, Platts OPR World Crude Table, Platts Global Alert/Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 1250, 1805, 2202, 2203, 2211, 2216, 2217, 2244, 2245, 2264, 2265, 2280, 2281, 2811 and 2812, LNG Alert pages 0050 and 0051, Platts Natural Gas Alert pages 1024 and 1069, and under the price database codes below:

Assessments Oman CFR North Asia Murban CFR North Asia Minas Singapore close Minas London close Cinta Singapore close Cinta London close Widuri Singapore close Widuri London close Minas FOB Indonesia $/

Platts to launch daily FOB Middle East bitumen assessments from Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, will launch daily VG30 and VG40 bitumen spot assessments for FOB Middle East, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
The new assessments will complement Platts' existing daily and weekly bitumen assessments, providing greater price transparency and information in the waterborne trade for the product in the Asia-Pacific and Middle East markets.
Platts will launch two new daily outright assessments and their respective monthly averages. These assessments will reflect bulk viscosity grade 30 and VG40 grade bitumen, as per IS 73:2013 specifications, loading 10-30 days forward from the date of publication in the Middle East.

Assessment FOB Middle East Bitumen VG30 FOB Middle East Bitumen VG40

Quantity

Laycan

2,500-10,000 mt, bulk 10-30 days forward

UoM $/mt

2,500-10,000 mt, bulk 10-30 days forward $/mt

Pricing information for material with other specifications, such as PEN 60/70, may be considered for the assessments, normalized to the proposed standard.
The assessments will reflect market value at the close of Asian trading at 4:30 pm Singapore time and follow the Singapore publication schedule. Bids, offers and trades reported throughout the day may be considered in the assessment process, with values normalized to the market close at 4:30 pm Singapore time.
This follows a proposal published Sept. 14 and available here. Please send any questions, comments or feedback to asia_products@spglobal.com, with a copy to pricegroup@spglobal.com. Platts proposes to amend rollover date of Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the rollover date of its Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close assessment to the first publication day of the month, from Dec. 1, 2026.
The proposed change, which follows Platts review of historical data sets, would align the ADB Strip at London close assessment with the current rollover date of its ADB Strip at Asia close assessment.
Currently, this assessment, which reflects the forward value of Dated Brent two months ahead of the date of publication at the 4:30 pm London close, rolls over on the 9th of the month.
The assessments affected by this change appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Asia Pacific/Arab Gulf Marketscan, Oilgram Price Report, Platts Global Alert/ Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 2206, 2213, 2215, 2217, 2219, 2260, 2261, 2262, 2263, 2264, 2265, 2266,

Platts revises measurement units, descriptions for FO Licht ethanol prices
The following Platts FOLicht ethanol prices have had their units of measurement and descriptions updated, as summarized below.
Cubic Meter Pure Alcohol (CPA) · Type: Volume Unit · Definition: A unit of volume that measures the volume of pure (100%) alcohol. One CBMPA equals 1 cubic meter (1,000 liters) of pure alcohol. Unlike a standard cubic meter (m³), which measures the total volume of any substance (liquid, gas, or solid), CBMPA refers specifically to the ethanol content, not the total liquid volume.

Units of measurements and descriptions updated for Platts FOLicht ethanol prices The following Platts FOLicht ethanol prices have had their Units of measurements and descriptions updated, as summarized below. Hectoliter Pure Alcohol (HPA) · Type: Volume Unit · Definition: A unit of volume that measures the volume of pure (100%) alcohol. One HLTPA equals 1 hectoliter (100 liters) of pure alcohol. Unlike a standard hectoliter (hL), which measures the total volume of any liquid, HLTPA refers only to the pure alcohol content, regardless of the concentration in the original beverage.

2-DAY TRAINING
Upstream Fundamentals and Economics
June 2-3 | Houston

Fossil fuels dominate the energy mix representing over 80% of global energy demand. With geopolitical tensions influencing supply volumes and lower price, the S&P Global Commodity Insights "Upstream Fundamentals and Economics" course will equip you with the insights and strategies needed to navigate a constantly changing environment, meet challenges with informed decisions, and drive business growth.
